Open r r -spin theory in genus one, and the Gelfand–Dikii wave function
Ran J. Tessler, Yizhen Zhao

Abstract
We construct the g = 1 g=1 sector of the open r r -spin theory, that is, an open r r -spin theory on the moduli space of cylinders. This is the second construction of a g > 0 g>0 open intersection theory, which includes descendents (the first is the all genus construction of the intersection theory on moduli of open Riemann surfaces with boundaries [ 23 , 32 ] , whose g = 1 g=1 case equals to the r = 2 r=2 case of our construction). Unlike the construction of [ 32 ] , in order to construct the r r -spin cylinder theory we had to overcome the foundational problem of dimension jump loci, which in analogous closed theories has been treated using virtual fundamental class techniques, that are currently absent in the open setting. For this reason our construction is much more involved, and relies on the point insertion technique developed in [ 29 , 30 ] . We prove that the open g = 1 g=1 potential equals, after a coordinate change, to the g = 1 g=1 part of the Gelfand–Dikii wave function, thus confirming a conjecture of [ 7 ] . We also prove that our g = 1 g=1 intersection numbers satisfy a g = 1 g=1 recursion, also predicted in [ 7 , 15 ] . This recursion is the g = 1 g=1 analogue of Solomon’s famous g = 0 g=0 Open WDVV equation [ 26 ] , with descendents, and is also the universal g = 1 g=1 recursion for F F -Cohomological field theories [ 1 ] . Again, this is first geometric construction which is not the g = 1 g=1 sector of [ 23 , 32 ] , proven to satisfy this universal recursion.



1
Introduction

1.1
Background on closed
r
-spin theory and Witten’s
r
-spin conjecture

1.1.1
Witten’s KdV conjecture

Witten’s conjecture
[
33
]
, proven by Kontsevich
[
21
]
, is one of the cornerstones in the modern study of the intersection theory on the moduli space of stable curves
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n},
as well as in the development of Gromov–Witten theory. Let
\psi_{1},\ldots,\psi_{n}\in H^{2}({\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n})
be the first Chern classes of the relative cotangent line bundles at the
n
marked points, and denote by

F^{c}(t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,\lambda):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geq 0,n\geq 1\\
2g-2+n>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0}\frac{\lambda^{2g-2}}{n!}\left(\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,n}}\psi_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots\psi_{n}^{d_{n}}\right)t_{d_{1}}\cdots t_{d_{n}}

the
closed potential
, a generating function of their integrals, which are called
intersection numbers
. The superscript ‘
c
’ stands for “closed”, as opposed to the open theory which is the focus of this paper.
\lambda
and
\{t_{i}\}_{i\geq 0}
are formal variables. The Witten–Kontsevich theorem reads that
\exp(F^{c})
is a tau-function of the Korteweg–de Vries (KdV) hierarchy, and equivalently that
\exp(F^{c})
satisfies the Virasoro constraints, which is some set of partial differential equations. Each of these equivalent formulations uniquely determines all intersection numbers.

1.1.2
Witten’s
r
-spin intersection theory

A year after the proposal of his original conjecture, Witten suggested a generalized conjecture
[
34
]
, in which the moduli space of curves is enhanced to the moduli space of
r
-spin curves. An
r
-spin structure on a smooth marked curve
(C;z_{1},\ldots,z_{n})
is a line bundle
S
together with an isomorphism

S^{\otimes r}\cong\omega_{C}\left(-\sum_{i=1}^{n}a_{i}[z_{i}]\right),

where
a_{i}\in\{0,1,\ldots,r-1\}
are called
twists
and
\omega_{C}
denotes the canonical bundle. There is a natural compactification
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}^{1/r}
of the moduli space of
r
-spin structures on smooth curves, and this space admits a virtual fundamental class
c_{W}
known as
Witten’s class
. In genus zero, Witten’s class is the Euler class of the
Witten bundle

(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S})^{\vee},
where
\pi:\mathcal{C}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}^{1/r}
is the universal curve,
\mathcal{S}
is the universal
r
-spin structure. In higher genus, on the other hand,
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}
is generally not a vector bundle, and defining the Witten’s class involved subtle virtual fundamental class techniques
[
24
,
11
,
22
,
14
,
10
]
.

The
(closed)
r
-spin intersection numbers
are defined by

\displaystyle\langle\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{a_{n}}_{d_{n}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},c}_{g}:=r^{1-g}\int_{\overline{\mathcal{M}}^{1/r}_{g,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\}}}\hskip-28.45274ptc_{W}\cup\psi_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots\psi_{n}^{d_{n}}.

(1.1)

The
closed
r
-spin potential

is the generating function of the closed
r
-spin intersection numbers

F^{\frac{1}{r},c}(t^{*}_{*},\lambda):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}g\geq 0,n\geq 1\\
2g-2+n>0\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{n}\leq r-1\\
d_{1},\ldots,d_{n}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{\lambda^{2g-2}}{n!}\langle\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{a_{n}}_{d_{n}}\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},c}_{g}t^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots t^{a_{n}}_{d_{n}},

where again
\lambda
and
t^{a}_{d}
for
0\leq a\leq r-1
and
d\geq 0
are formal variables.

1.1.3
The Gelfand–Dikii (GD) integrable hierarchy and the
r
-spin conjecture

Recall that a
pseudo-differential operator

A
is a Laurent series

A=\sum_{n=-\infty}^{m}a_{n}(T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,\lambda)\partial_{x}^{n},

where
m
is an integer,
T_{i}
for
i\geq 1,~\lambda
and
\partial_{x}
are formal variables, and
a_{n}(T_{1},T_{2},\ldots,\lambda)\in{\mathbb{C}}[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}][[T_{1},T_{2},\ldots]]
. The space of pseudo differential operators is a non-commutative associative algebra, in which the multiplication
\circ
is induced from the usual product on
{\mathbb{C}}[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}][[T_{1},T_{2},\ldots]]
and

\partial_{x}^{k}\circ f:=\sum_{l=0}^{\infty}\frac{k(k-1)\ldots(k-l+1)}{l!}\frac{\partial^{l}f}{\partial x^{l}}\partial_{x}^{k-l},

where
k
is an integer,
f\in\mathbb{C}[\lambda,\lambda^{-1}][[T_{*}]]
, and the variable
x
is identified with
T_{1}
. Let
r\geq 2
be any integer, and
A
a pseudo-differential operator of the form

A=\partial_{x}^{r}+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}a_{n}\partial_{x}^{r-n},

then
A
has a unique
r
th root, meaning a unique pseudo-differential operator
A^{\frac{1}{r}}
of the form

A^{\frac{1}{r}}=\partial_{x}+\sum_{n=0}^{\infty}b_{n}\partial_{x}^{-n}

satisfying
\left(A^{\frac{1}{r}}\right)^{r}=A
.

Let
r\geq 2
, and consider the operator

L:=\partial_{x}^{r}+\sum_{i=0}^{r-2}f_{i}\partial_{x}^{i},\quad f_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}[\lambda^{\pm 1}][[T_{*}]].

It is not hard to check that for any
n\geq 1
, the commutator
[(L^{n/r})_{+},L]
has the form
\sum_{i=0}^{r-2}h_{i}\partial_{x}^{i}
with
h_{i}\in{\mathbb{C}}[\lambda^{\pm 1}][[T_{*}]]
, where
(\cdot)_{+}
denotes the part of a pseudo-differential operator composed of the non-negative powers of
\partial_{x}
. The
r
th Gelfand–Dikii hierarchy
, sometimes called the
r
KdV hierarchy
is the system of partial differential equations for
f_{0},f_{1},\ldots,f_{r-2}
given by:

\frac{\partial L}{\partial T_{n}}=\lambda^{n-1}[(L^{n/r})_{+},L],\quad n\geq 1.

(1.2)

Denote by
L
the solution of the system specified by the initial condition

L|_{T_{\geq 2}=0}=\partial_{x}^{r}+\lambda^{-r}rx,

(1.3)

Witten’s
r
-spin conjecture states that
\frac{\partial F^{\frac{1}{r},c}}{\partial t^{r-1}_{d}}=0
for
d\geq 0
(the
Ramond vanishing phenomenon
) and that under the change of variables

\displaystyle T_{k}=\frac{1}{(-r)^{\frac{3k}{2(r+1)}-\frac{1}{2}-d}k!_{r}}t^{a}_{d},\quad 0\leq a\leq r-2,\quad d\geq 0,

(1.4)

where
k=a+1+rd
and

k!_{r}:=\prod_{i=0}^{d}(a+1+ri),

it holds that

\res L^{n/r}=\lambda^{1-n}\frac{\partial^{2}F^{\frac{1}{r},c}}{\partial T_{1}\partial T_{n}}

whenever
n\geq 1
is not divisible by
r
, and
\res L^{n/r}
denotes the coefficient of
\partial_{x}^{-1}
in
L^{n/r}
.

In the language of integrable hierarchies this means that
\exp(F^{\frac{1}{r},c})
becomes, after a simple change of variables, a
tau-function
of the
r
th Gelfand–Dikii hierarchy.

This result was proven by Faber–Shadrin–Zvonkine
[
13
]
. The case
r=2
is equivalent to the original conjecture of Witten. Witten’s
r
-spin construction initiated the study of Fan–Jarvis–Ruan–Witten
[
14
]
theory of quantum singularities.

1.1.4
Closed extended
g=0

r
-spin theory

[
20
]
showed that the genus-zero
r
-spin theory can be extended to allow a single marked point of twist
-1
. This theory was further studied in
[
5
]
, and was shown to be related to the Gelfand–Dikii hierarchy as well. We denote the intersection numbers in this extended theory by
\langle\cdots\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},ext}.
Extended intersection numbers which include a
-1
twist need not to satisfy the Ramond vanishing, and are crucial for writing recursions for open intersection numbers.

1.2
Open
r
-spin theory, and the open analogue of Witten’s
r
-spin conjecture

1.2.1
The open analogue of Witten’s KdV conjecture

The study of intersection theory on the moduli spaces of surfaces with boundary was initiated by Pandharipande, Solomon, and the third author in
[
23
]
(the disk case) and
[
25
,
32
]
(the
g>0
case). These works consider the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}
, which parametrizes tuples
(\Sigma;x_{1},\ldots,x_{k};z_{1},\ldots,z_{l})
in which
\Sigma
is a stable Riemann surface with boundary, endowed with an additional structure called
graded spin structure
,
x_{i}\in\partial\Sigma
are boundary marked points, and
z_{j}\in\Sigma\setminus\partial\Sigma
are internal marked points. When
g=0
the grading data is trivial and
\Sigma
is just a stable marked disk. The intersection numbers on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}
, which should be thought of as integrals of
\psi
-classes at the internal marked points, were constructed and hence the
open potential

F^{o}(t_{0},t_{1},\ldots,s,\lambda)
was defined as a direct generalization of
F^{c}
; the new formal variable
s
tracks the number of boundary marked points.
[
23
]
conjectured an open analogue of Witten’s conjecture for these intersection numbers: That the open potential satisfies open analogues of the Virasoro constraints and of the KdV equations. The proof of these conjectures was obtained by combining the results of
[
3
,
32
,
8
]
. A survey for physicists can be found in
[
12
]
.

There were two conceptual difficulties in defining the open intersection numbers. The first was that the naïve moduli of Riemann surfaces with boundary has no canonical orientation, and is sometimes non orientable, when
g>0,
what complicates the definition of integrals. The other, more intricate, problem is the presence of real codimension
1
boundaries for the moduli. Because of these boundaries any reasonable definition of integral should involve an imposition of boundary conditions. The inclusion of graded spin structures solved these problems. The moduli of graded spin structure is an oriented orbifold cover of the naïve moduli of surfaces with boundaries, see
[
32
, §5]
. Moreover, this structure picks, for every stratum of nodal surface, and each boundary node, a distinguished
illegal
half node. The
forgetful boundary conditions
on the
\psi_{i}
“classes” are essentially that on a boundary stratum the section of
\psi_{i}
is pulled back from the moduli which parametrizes surfaces obtained by normalizing the nodes of the stratum, and forgetting the illegal side. See
[
32
, §2]
for details.

1.2.2
Genus-zero open
r
-spin intersection theory

An open analogue of Witten’s
r
-spin theory was constructed in genus
0,
the disk case in
[
6
,
4
]
and
[
29
,
30
]
.
The work
[
6
]
constructed the moduli space of
graded
r
-spin disks

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\}}^{1/r},
which parametrizes stable marked disks with a spin structure and a grading. The twists
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}
of internal points again ranged in
\{0,\ldots,r-1\},
but boundary marked points were required to have twist
r-2.

[
6
]
also defined an open analogue of Witten’s bundle
\mathcal{W}=(R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S})^{\vee}
, again in
g=0
and for the same objects, and proved that the bundle is canonically relatively oriented.

[
4
]
constructed an intersection theory for graded
r
-spin disks. As in the spinless case described above, the geometry enters the most in defining meaningful boundary conditions.
It turns out that for some strata, or, equivalently, some types of boundary nodes, the forgetful boundary condition scheme still applies. But for most of the boundary strata it fails. The main new geometric insight of
[
4
]
is the discovery of a hidden positivity structure, which again builds on the grading. This has led to imposing the
positivity boundary conditions
on the remaining boundaries.
The open, genus
0

r
-spin intersection numbers are defined by

\langle\tau_{d_{1}}^{a_{1}}\cdots\tau^{a_{l}}_{d_{l}}\sigma^{k}\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}:=\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{1/r}_{0,k,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\}}}e\left(\mathcal{W}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\text{canonical}}\right),

(1.5)

where, on a manifold with boundary
M
, the notation
e(E,s)
denotes the Euler class of
E
relative to the boundary condition
s
of
E|_{\partial M}\to\partial M
.
{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r}}_{0,k,\vec{a}}}
is a modification of
{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r}}_{0,k,\vec{a}}}
obtained by removing certain boundary strata.
1
1

1

One can also work directly on
{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r}}_{0,k,\vec{a}}}
but then the definition becomes more technically complicated.
Finally, the section
\mathbf{s}_{\text{canonical}}
is any choice of nowhere-vanishing section of
\mathcal{W}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}}|_{\partial{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r}}_{0,k,\vec{a}}}}
which satisfies both the forgetful, and the positivity boundary conditions. It was shown that any two such choices of boundary sections yield the same intersection number.
It was also shown that in
g=0
these numbers are non zero only if

2\sum_{i=1}^{l}d_{i}+\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{l}a_{i}+k(r-2)+(g-1)(r-2)}{r}=2l+k+3g-3.

(1.6)

In particular,

{2\sum_{i=1}^{l}a_{i}+k(r-2)+(g-1)(r-2)}=0~\mod~r.

(1.7)

and

\frac{2\sum_{i=1}^{l}a_{i}+k(r-2)+(g-1)(r-2)}{r}=k+g-1~\mod~2

(1.8)

hold when setting
g=0.

The genus
0
open
r
-spin potential is the generating function

F^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}(t^{*}_{*},s):=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k,l\geq 0\\
k+2l>2\end{subarray}}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\leq r-1\\
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\frac{1}{k!l!}\left<\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{a_{l}}_{d_{l}}\sigma^{k}\right>^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}t^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots t^{a_{l}}_{d_{l}}s^{k}.

The goal of
[
29
,
30
]
was to define intersection theories which allow more types of boundary twists.
The new geometric insight was that the forgetful boundary conditions should be replaced by the new
point insertion boundary conditions
.

[
30
]
defined
\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor
open
r
-spin intersection theories, indexed by
\mathfrak{h}\in\{0,1,\ldots,\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor-1\}.
The
\mathfrak{h}=0
zero was shown in
[
30
, §5]
to carry the same information as the open
r
-spin theory of
[
4
]
, and a minor modification of it produced the exact same intersection numbers as
[
4
]
, given in (
1.5
), see
[
30
, Remark 5.7]
.
This last intersection theory, defined via the point insertion and positivity boundary conditions, is the starting point of this work.

1.2.3
The
r
GD wave function and the conjecture of
[
7
]

With
L
as in §
1.1.3
, let
\Phi(T_{*},\lambda)
be the solution of the system of equations

\displaystyle\frac{\partial\Phi}{\partial T_{n}}=\lambda^{n-1}(L^{n/r})_{+}\Phi,\quad n\geq 1,

(1.9)

which satisfies the initial condition

\left.\Phi\right|_{T_{\geq 2}=0}=1.

The system of differential equations (
1.9
) for the function
\Phi
coincides with the system of differential equations for the wave function of the KP hierarchy.
This function
\Phi
, for
r=2
was first studied in
[
3
]
. For general
r
it was first studied in
[
2
]
, where the authors found an explicit formula for
\Phi
in terms of the wave function.

Let
\phi:=\log\Phi
and expand it as a power series in
\lambda:

\phi=\sum_{g\in\mathbb{Z}}\lambda^{g-1}\phi_{g},\quad\phi_{g}\in{\mathbb{C}}[[T_{*}]].

While
F^{\frac{1}{r},c}_{0}
, depends only on the variables
t^{0}_{d},\ldots,t^{r-2}_{d}
, the function
F^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}
depends also on
t^{r-1}_{d}
and
s
. We relate
T_{mr}
and
t^{r-1}_{m-1}
via

\displaystyle T_{mr}=\frac{1}{(-r)^{\frac{m(r-2)}{2(r+1)}}m!r^{m}}t^{r-1}_{m-1},\quad m\geq 1.

(1.10)

The main algebraic result of
[
4
]
is:

Theorem 1.1
.

It holds that

\displaystyle F^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{-r}}\phi_{0}\big|_{t^{r-1}_{d}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{-r}}(t^{r-1}_{d}-r\delta_{d,0}s)}-\frac{1}{\sqrt{-r}}\phi_{0}\big|_{t^{r-1}_{d}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{-r}}t^{r-1}_{d}}.

(1.11)

This provides the open
r
-spin version of Witten’s conjecture in genus zero. A mirror theoretic interpretation of the invariants constructed in
[
4
]
appears in
[
16
]
.

In the case
r=2

[
3
]
proved an explicit formula for the spinless open potential
F^{o}
of
[
23
,
32
]
in terms of the function
\phi=\log\Phi
.

We should point out that at the moment no analogous results are known or conjectured for the
\lfloor r/2\rfloor
open
r
-spin theories constructed in
[
29
,
30
]
, even though all
g=0
intersection numbers of these theories are fully calculated in
[
30
]
, and despite seemingly related predictions from
[
18
]
and implicitly also from
[
19
]
.

[
7
]
conjectured an all genus genus generalization of Theorem
1.1
. More precisely, they conjectured the following.

Conjecture 1

(The rGD wave function conjecture of
[
7
]
)
.

For every genus
g\geq 1
, there is a geometric construction of open
r
-spin intersection numbers

\displaystyle\langle\tau^{\alpha_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{\alpha_{l}}_{d_{l}}\sigma^{k}\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}.

(1.12)

Moreover, if we define the genus
g
potential
F_{g}^{\frac{1}{r},o}(t^{*}_{*},s)
by

F_{g}^{\frac{1}{r},o}(t^{*}_{*},s):=\sum_{l,k\geq 0}\frac{1}{l!k!}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}0\leq\alpha_{1},\ldots,\alpha_{l}\leq r-1\\
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}\geq 0\end{subarray}}\langle\tau^{\alpha_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots\tau^{\alpha_{l}}_{d_{l}}\sigma^{k}\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}t^{\alpha_{1}}_{d_{1}}\cdots t^{\alpha_{l}}_{d_{l}}s^{k}.

(1.13)

Then for every
g\geq 1
, we have

F^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}=\left.(-r)^{\frac{g-1}{2}}\phi_{g}\right|_{t^{r-1}_{d}\mapsto\frac{1}{\sqrt{-r}}(t^{r-1}_{d}-\delta_{d,0}rs)}.

(1.14)

Remark 1.2
.

In open Gromov--Witten theory, unlike the closed theory, not much is known or even conjectured about higher-genus invariants. The conjecture above is on of the few conjectures that describes a full, all-genus open Gromov--Witten theory, and also one of the few conjectures that relate the potential to an integrable hierarchy.
2
2

2

As mentioned, an analogous conjecture was made in
[
23
]
for the
r=2
case, and it was proven in
[
8
]
. In
[
9
]
, the full stationary open Gromov–Witten theory for maps to
(\mathbb{CP}^{1},\mathbb{RP}^{1})
is conjectured.

1.2.4
The universal
g=1
open topological recursion relations, string and dilaton relations

In
[
26
]
Solomon showed that certain
g=0
open GW theories satisfy a universal relation which he termed the
open WDVV
. Also the genus
0

r
-spin theory of
[
6
,
4
,
7
]
can be shown to satisfy the open WDVV. While not all open GW and open FJRW theories are expected to satisfy the open WDVV (e.g. the rank
2
Fermat theory studied in
[
17
]
does not satisfy this relation), a large class of open GW theories, which include the OGW theory of
(\mathbb{CP}^{n},\mathbb{RP}^{n})
for
n
odd
[
27
]
, do satisfy.

In
[
1
]
Alexandrov, Basalaev and Buryak construct an algebraic extension of the open potentials of theories satisfying the open WDVV to higher genus and to include descendents. They show that their extensions satisfy certain universal equations: the
open string
and
open dilaton
equations for all genus, and
g=0,1

topological recursion relations
. See also
[
15
]
.
They conjecture that there exist geometric constructions of these extensions. We refer the reader to their paper for further details.

In the case of
r
-spin theory, if we denote by
F^{\frac{1}{r}}(t_{\star}^{\star},s)
the conjectural extension, and
F^{\frac{1}{r}}_{g}
is its genus
g
component, then we can write these equations concretely in terms of intersection numbers as follows. We will assume in all cases that the left hand side of the equation satisfies (
1.6
).

(a)

(Boundary marked point
g=0
TRR) Suppose
l,k\geq 1
. Then

\displaystyle\left<\tau_{d_{1}+1}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right>^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=-1}^{r-2}\sum_{S\sqcup R=\{2,\ldots,l\}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{a}\tau_{d_{1}}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i\in S}\tau_{d_{i}}^{a_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},{ext}}_{0}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{r-2-a}\prod_{i\in R}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\sqcup R=\{2,\ldots,l\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k-1\end{subarray}}\binom{k-1}{k_{1}}\left\langle\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\prod_{i\in S}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{1}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}\left\langle\prod_{i\in R}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{2}+2}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}.

(b)

(Internal marked point
g=0
TRR) Suppose
l\geq 2
. Then

\displaystyle\left<\tau_{d_{1}+1}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i=2}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right>^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}=

\displaystyle\sum_{a=-1}^{r-2}\sum_{S\sqcup R=\{3,\ldots,l\}}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{a}\tau_{d_{1}}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i\in S}\tau_{d_{i}}^{a_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},{ext}}_{0}\left\langle\tau_{0}^{r-2-a}\tau^{a_{2}}_{d_{2}}\prod_{i\in R}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}+

\displaystyle+\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}S\sqcup R=\{3,\ldots,l\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\end{subarray}}\binom{k}{k_{1}}\left\langle\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\prod_{i\in S}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{1}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}\left\langle\tau^{a_{2}}_{d_{2}}\prod_{i\in R}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{2}+1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}.

(c)

(Genus
1
TRR)

\begin{split}\left\langle\tau_{d_{1}+1}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i\in[l]\setminus\{1\}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{1}\hskip-5.69046pt=&\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J_{1}\sqcup J_{2}=[l]\setminus\{1\}\\
-1\leq a\leq r-2\end{subarray}}\hskip-2.84544pt\left\langle\tau_{0}^{a}\tau_{d_{1}}^{a_{1}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\tau_{d_{i}}^{a_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},\text{ext}}_{0}\hskip-2.84544pt\left\langle\tau_{0}^{r-2-a}\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{1}\\
&+\hskip-2.84544pt\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}J_{1}\sqcup J_{2}=[l]\setminus\{1\}\\
k_{1}+k_{2}=k\end{subarray}}\hskip-2.84544pt\binom{k}{k_{1}}\left\langle\tau^{a_{1}}_{d_{1}}\prod_{i\in J_{1}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{1}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}\hskip-2.84544pt\left\langle\prod_{i\in J_{2}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k_{2}+1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{1}\\
&+\frac{1}{2}\left\langle\prod_{i\in[l]}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k+1}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{0}.\end{split}

(1.15)

(d)

(Open String)
Assume
2g-2+k+2l>0
then

\left\langle\tau^{0}_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}=\sum_{j=1}^{l}\left\langle\tau^{a_{j}}_{d_{j}-1}\prod_{i=1,i\neq j}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g},

(1.16)

where an intersection number which includes
\tau^{*}_{-1}
is defined to be
0.

(e)

(Open Dilaton)
Assume
2g-2+k+2l>0
, then

\left\langle\tau^{1}_{0}\prod_{i=1}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}=(g+l+k-1)\left\langle\prod_{i=1}^{l}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{g}

(1.17)

The first two equations were proven to hold in
[
4
]
.

1.3
This work: An
r
-spin cylinder intersection theory

In this work we move up to genus
1,
and construct the
g=1
open
r
-spin theory, or equivalently the
open
r
-spin cylinder theory
. This is the first rigorous construction of an open FJRW theory in positive genus.
The difficulties which appeared in the construction of the
r
-spin disk theories, that is the orientation problem and the choice of boundary conditions, appear in the cylinder theory as well, and are more challenging to overcome. In addition, we encounter a new difficulty: a dimension jump in the Witten “bundle”. Thus, it is a true bundle only on the complement of a subspace.

To treat the dimension jump problem we first identified the dimension jump loci in the moduli, and then restricted our attention to sections of our bundles-of-interest which have a prescribed non vanishing behaviour near these loci.
This is essentially equivalent to excluding (neighbourhoods of) these loci from the moduli space, thus obtaining new boundaries, and imposing prescribed boundary conditions.

The orientation problem has been solved in
[
29
, §3]
, where it was shown that away from the dimension jump loci the Witten bundle has a canonical relative orientation, which allows us to integrate.

A key difficulty in defining boundary conditions was that the positivity of the Witten bundle’s sections, which played a central role in constructing the
g=0
theory, proving independence of choices for intersection numbers, and calculating these numbers, surprisingly breaks in
g=1.

This expected positivity has lead the first-named-author to anticipate, in
[
7
]
, that the (back then) yet-to-be-defined open
r
-spin theory in
g=1
would satisfy the genus-one topological recursion relation Theorem
1.4
below.

But there is a cure for this problem, which comes from the relative cotangent lines
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}.
Dimension-versus-Rank considerations (
1.6
) show that only intersection numbers which contain at least one
\psi_{i}
class may be non zero. Miraculously, there exist completely new, natural, boundary conditions for the
\psi_{i}
classes at those boundaries that the expected positivity does not hold. Interestingly, in order to define these new boundary conditions consistently we need to work with the point-insertion analogue of the BCT
r
-spin theory mentioned above. We do not know if this is a fundamental problem or just a technicality. We refer to sections of direct sums of the Witten bundle and relative cotangent lines satisfying the above boundary conditions as
canonical
.

To summarize, combining the above ideas we have shown (see Definition
4.13
and Theorem
5.6
for a precise statement)

Theorem 1.3
.

Assume that the twists
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}
and numbers of descendents
d_{1},\ldots,d_{l}
satisfy (
1.6
) with
g=1
. We can define an integral

\int_{\widetilde{\mathcal{PM}}^{1/r}_{1,k,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\}}}e\left(\mathcal{W}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}},\mathbf{s}_{\text{canonical}}\right),

where
{\widetilde{\mathcal{PM}}^{1/r}_{1,k,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\}}}
is the point insertion moduli space of
r
-spin cylinders with internal points twisted by
a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}
and
k
boundary markings twisted
r-2
, after removing certain boundary strata, and
\mathbf{s}_{\text{canonical}}
is an arbitrary choice of nowhere-vanishing canonical section of
\mathcal{W}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}}|_{\partial{\widetilde{\mathcal{PM}}^{1/r}_{1,k,\{a_{1},\ldots,a_{l}\}}}}
.

Moreover, the integral is independent of the choice of the non-vanishing canonical
\mathbf{s}_{\text{canonical}}.

We denote the above integral by
\left\langle\tau_{d_{1}}^{a_{1}}\ldots\tau^{a_{l}}_{d_{l}}\sigma^{k}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},o}_{1}.

These numbers are taken to be
0
if (
1.6
) for
g=1
fails.

The above intersection numbers can also be calculated. The main algebraic results are

Theorem 1.4
.

The open
g=1
TRR (
1.15
) holds, whenever the left-hand side, satisfies (
1.6
).

The proof is very different from proofs of
g=0
TRR and
g=0
open WDVV which have appeared in the literature
[
23
,
4
,
30
,
27
]
and relies strongly on
g=1
properties.
This is the first
g=1
open model which is proven to satisfy the universal
g=1
TRR, except for the special case
r=2,
which can be shown algebraically to satisfy TRR by combining the proof of the open analogue of Witten’s conjecture
[
8
]
and
[
7
, Theorem 1]
.

Theorem 1.5
.

The potential of the genus-one open
r
-spin numbers, defined via (
1.13
) for
g=1,
satisfies the
g=1
case of the rGD wave function conjecture
1
.

Theorem 1.6
.

Assume
k+2l>0,
then the
g=1
Open String equation (
1.16
) and the Open Dilaton equation (
1.17
) hold as long as their left hand side satisfies (
1.6
).

Again, with the exception of the special case
r=2
studied in
[
31
,
8
]
this is the only work so far which has shown relation between an open theory and an integrable hierarchy in
g>0,
as well as the first work to have proven the open string and dilaton for
g>0.

1.4
Discussion: Possible generalizations

This works suggests several natural research avenues.

•

g>1:

It is desired to construct the
g>1
sectors of open
r
spin as well, and to prove Conjecture
1
for them. All difficulties which were solved in this work also make their appearance in the
g>1
cases, in an amplified manner. In particular the dimension jump loci are less understood, hence it may be unavoidable to wait for the development of virtual cycle machinery.

•

Higher rank open FJRWs:
[
17
]
constructed higher rank open FJRW theories. Our construction does not extend to this setting, since it may include primary
g=1
intersection numbers, but our construction leans on the presence of
\psi_{i}
classes. Still, this obstacle might be overcame in different ways. We expect the resulting intersection numbers to be subject to
wall crossings
, like in
[
17
]
, but that some polynomial combinations of them will be invariant, and satisfy
g=1
TRR, which is
structurally different
from the TRR of Theorem
1.4
.

•

Theories with more types of boundary insertions:
[
30
]
constructs
\lfloor\frac{r}{2}\rfloor
open
r
-spin theories with more possible boundary twists. It is natural to generalize the construction of this paper to this setting, and to seek TRRs for them.

•

Open Gromov–Witten (OGW) theories:

Finally, a very natural generalization is to Open GW theories. Also in the Open GW case dimension jumps should be milder in
g=1
than in
g>1.

As mentioned above, large class of
g=0
Open GW theories are subject to Solomon’s OWDVV
[
26
,
27
]
. We expect that for these theories there should be a
g=1
extension which satisfies the analogue of our
g=1
TRR, in which the boundary conditions for the
\psi_{i}
classes should resemble those we construct here.

1.5
Plan of the paper

The structure of the paper is as follows. In §
2
we review the open
r
-spin structures, their moduli spaces, the Witten bundles, and the point insertion technique studied in
[
6
,
29
]
. In §
3
, we focus on the relative cotangent line bundles
\mathbb{L}
: we study the relation between them and construct special sections of them. In §
4
we introduce the notion of canonical sections and define the genus-one open
r
-spin intersection numbers as the zero count of a canonical section. In §
5
we show that the genus-one open
r
-spin intersection numbers are independent of the choice of the canonical section, and prove that they satisfy the genus-one open Topological Recursion Relations. In §
6
we prove the existence of canonical sections.

1.6
Acknowledgements

R.T. thanks Tyler Kelly, Jake Solomon and Sasha Buryak for interesting discussion related to the content of this work. R.T. and Y.Z. were supported by the ISF (grant No. 1729/23).

2
Review of graded
r
-spin disks and cylinders

In this section, we review the definition of graded
r
-spin disks and cylinders, their moduli space, the relevant bundles and the point insertion technique. More details can be found in
[
6
,
29
]
.

Throughout the paper we will use the notation
[N],
where
N
is a natural number, to denote the set
\{1,2,\dots,N\}.

2.1
The moduli space of graded
r
-spin surfaces

The main objects of study in this paper are genus-zero and genus-one marked Riemann surfaces with boundary; we view them as arising from closed spheres and torus with an orientation reversing involution. More precisely, a
nodal marked surface
is defined as a tuple

(C,\phi,\Sigma,\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I},\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B},m^{I},m^{B}),

in which

•

C
is a nodal orbifold Riemann surface, which may be composed of disconnected components, and has isotropy only at special points;

•

\phi:C\to C
is an anti-holomorphic involution which, from a topological perspective, realizes the coarse underlying Riemann surface
|C|
as the union of two Riemann surfaces,
\Sigma
and
\overline{\Sigma}=\phi(\Sigma),
glued along their common subset
\text{Fix}(|\phi|)
;

•

\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I}\subset C
consists of distinct
internal marked points
(or
internal tails
) whose images in
|C|
lie in
\Sigma\setminus\text{Fix}(|\phi|)
. We denote by
\overline{z_{i}}:=\phi(z_{i})
their
conjugate marked points
;

•

\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B}\subset\text{Fix}(\phi)
consists of distinct
boundary marked points
(or
boundary tails
) whose images in
|C|
lie in
{\partial}\Sigma
;

•

m^{I}
(respectively
m^{B}
) is a marking of
I
and (respectively
B
),
i.e.
a one-to-one correspondence between
I
(respectively
m^{B}
) and
\left[\lvert I\rvert\right]
(respectively
[\lvert B\rvert]
).

The genus of a nodal marked surface is the sum of the genus of each connected component of
C
. We call a nodal marked surface a disk if it is genus-zero, and a cylinder if it is genus-one.
We say that a nodal marked surface is
stable
if each genus-zero irreducible component has at least three special points, and each genus-one irreducible component has at least one special point.

Remark 2.1
.

If
\text{Fix}(|\phi|)=\emptyset
, then
\Sigma
is a closed nodal orbifold Riemann surface, and
C=\Sigma\sqcup\overline{\Sigma}
is a disjoint union. Notice that in this case the genus is always even, and therefore for a genus-one nodal marked surface we always have
\text{Fix}(|\phi|)\neq\emptyset
.

A node of a nodal marked surface can be internal, boundary or contracted boundary; on genus-one connected component, an internal or boundary node can be separating or non-separating, as illustrated in Figure
1
by shading
\Sigma\subseteq|C|
in each case. Note that
{\partial}\Sigma\subset\text{Fix}(|\phi|)
is a union of circles, and
\text{Fix}(|\phi|)\setminus{\partial}\Sigma
is the union of the contracted boundary nodes.

(a)

separating, internal

(b)

non-separating, internal

(c)

separating, boundary

(d)

non-separating, boundary

(e)

contracted boundary

Figure 1
:
The five types of nodes on a nodal marked cylinder.

A
rooted nodal marked disk
is a connected genus-zero nodal marked surface together with a choice of a distinguished boundary marked point (called the root). A
2-rooted nodal marked disk
is a connected genus-zero nodal marked surface together with a choice of two distinguished boundary marked points (called the roots). An
anchored nodal marked surface
is a nodal marked surface together with a
\phi
-invariant choice of a distinguished internal marked point (called the
anchor
) on each connected component
C^{\prime}
of
C
that is disjoint from the set
\text{Fix}(\phi)
. We denote by
Anc\subseteq I
the set of indexes of anchors lying on
\Sigma
. Notice that for a genus-zero or genus-one anchored nodal marked surface, the anchors can only be on the genus-zero connected components.

Remark 2.2
.

We focus mainly on cylinders with boundaries and their degenerations,
i.e.
connected genus-one nodal marked surfaces. (Disconnected) surfaces with marked surfaces as component will usually appear as the result of normalizations at nodes (and later also from the point insertion method we shall define). The roots and anchors help us keep track of these normalizations:

-

If we normalize at a separating boundary node of a connected nodal cylinder, then we get two connected components, one genus-zero and one genus-one. The genus-zero component is a rooted nodal marked disk if we regard the half-node from normalization on the genus-zero side as a root.

-

If we normalize at a non-separating boundary node of a connected nodal cylinder, then we get a connected nodal marked disk, which is a 2-rooted nodal marked disk if we regard the two half-nodes from normalization as roots.

-

If we normalize at a pair (preserved by
\phi
) of separating internal nodes of a connected nodal surface with non-empty
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
, then we get three connected components, one intersects
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
and a pair (preserved by
\psi
) disjoint from
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
. We choose the half-nodes from normalization on the sides disjoint from
\operatorname{Fix}(\phi)
to be the anchors.

We use two similar terminologies ‘root’ and ‘anchor’, where ‘root’ will only be used for half-nodes on open components and ‘anchor’ is reserved for internal half-nodes on a closed surface. The usage of ‘anchor’ is also consistent with the terminology in
[
6
,
4
,
29
,
30
]
.

2.1.1
Open
r
-spin structures

Let
C
be an anchored nodal marked surface with
order-
r
cyclic isotopy groups at markings and nodes, a
r
-spin structure
on
C
is

•

an orbifold complex line bundle
L
on
C
,

•

an isomorphism

\kappa:L^{\otimes r}\cong\omega_{C,log}:=\omega_{C}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\left(\sum_{i\in I}[z_{i}]+\sum_{i\in I}[\overline{z_{i}}]+\sum_{j\in B}[x_{j}]\right),

where
[p]
is the degree-
1/r
divisor for each marking
p
.

•

an involution
\widetilde{\phi}:L\to L
lifting
\phi
.

The local isotopy of
L
at a point
p
is characterized by an integer
\operatorname{mult}_{p}(L)\in\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
in the following way: the local structure of the total space of
L
near
p
is
[\mathbb{C}^{2}/(\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z})]
, where the canonical generator
\xi\in\mathbb{Z}/r\mathbb{Z}
acts by
\xi\cdot(x,y)=(\xi x,\xi^{\operatorname{mult}_{p}(L)}y)
.

We denote by
RI\subseteq I
and
RB\subseteq B
the subsets of internal and boundary marked points
p
satisfying
\operatorname{mult}_{p}(L)=0
. An
associated twisted r-spin structure

S
is defined by

S:=L\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(-\sum_{i\in\widetilde{RI}}r[z_{i}]-\sum_{i\in\widetilde{RI}}r[\overline{z_{i}}]-\sum_{j\in RB}r[x_{j}]\right),

where
\widetilde{RI}\subseteq RI
is a subset satisfying
\widetilde{RI}=RI\setminus Anc
.

For an internal marked point
z_{i}
, we define the
internal twist
at
z_{i}
to be
a_{i}:=\operatorname{mult}_{z_{i}}(L)-1
if
i\in I\setminus\widetilde{RI}

and
a_{i}:=r-1
if
i\in\widetilde{RI}
. For a boundary marked point
x_{i}
, we define the
boundary twist
at
x_{j}
as
b_{j}:=\operatorname{mult}_{x_{j}}(L)-1
if
i\in B\setminus{RB}
and as
b_{j}:=r-1
if
j\in{RB}
. Note that all the marked points with twist
-1
are indexed by
RI\setminus\widetilde{RI}\subseteq Anc
. When the surface
C
is smooth, the coarse underlying bundle
|S|
over the coarse underlying sphere
|C|
satisfies

|S|^{\otimes r}\cong\omega_{|C|}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\left(-\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}[z_{i}]-\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}[\overline{z_{i}}]-\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}[x_{j}]\right),

here
[p]
is the degree-one divisor on the coarse
|C|
corresponding to each marking
p
.

Observation 2.3
.

A connected nodal marked disk admits a twisted
r
-spin structure with twists
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
if and only if

\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}-r+2}{r}\in\mathbb{Z},

(2.1)

and a connected nodal marked cylinder admits a twisted
r
-spin structure with twists
a_{i}
and
b_{j}
if and only if

\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}}{r}\in\mathbb{Z}.

(2.2)

These formulae are the specializations to our setting of the more well-known fact
[
34
]
: a (closed) connected nodal marked genus-
g
curve admits a twisted
r
-spin structure twists
a_{i}
if and only if

\frac{\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+(g-1)(r-2)}{r}\in{\mathbb{Z}}.

(2.3)

We can extend the definition of twists to
half-nodes
. Let
n:\widehat{C}\to C
be the normalization morphism. For a half-node
q\in\widehat{C}
, we denote by
\sigma_{0}(q)
the other half-node corresponding to the same node
n(q)
as
q
. The isotopies of
n^{*}L
at
q
and
\sigma_{0}(q)
satisfy

\operatorname{mult}_{q}(n^{*}L)+\operatorname{mult}_{\sigma_{0}(q)}(n^{*}L)\equiv 0\mod r.

It is important to note that
n^{*}S
may not be a twisted
r
-spin structure (associated with
n^{*}L
), because its connected components could potentially contain too many marked points with twist
-1
(note that marked points with twist
-1
are anchors). Nevertheless, in genus zero or one, there is a canonical way to choose a minimal subset
\mathcal{R}
of the half-nodes making

\widehat{S}:=n^{*}S\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\left(-\sum_{q\in\mathcal{R}}r[q]\right)

(2.4)

a twisted
r
-spin structure: denoting by
\mathcal{T}
the set of half-nodes
q
of
C
where
\operatorname{mult}_{q}(n^{*}L)=0
, we define

\mathcal{A}:=\left\{q\in\mathcal{T}\colon\begin{array}[]{ccc}\text{$n(q)$ is an internal node; after normalizing the }\\
\text{node $n(q)$, the half-node $\sigma_{0}(q)$ belongs to a connected }\\
\text{component meeting $\text{Fix}(\phi)$ or containing an anchor.}\end{array}\right\}

and set
\mathcal{R}:=\mathcal{T}\setminus\mathcal{A}
. Note that elements of
\mathcal{R}
always come from a separating internal node and lie on the genus-zero irreducible component disjoint from
\text{Fix}(\phi)
, see
[
6
, §2.3]
for more details.
We define
c_{t}
,
the twist of
S
at a half-node

h_{t}
, as
c_{t}:=\operatorname{mult}_{h_{t}}(n^{*}L)-1
if
h_{t}\notin\mathcal{R}
and as
c_{t}:=r-1
if
h_{t}\in\mathcal{R}
.

For each irreducible component
C_{l}
of
\widehat{C}
with half-nodes
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in N_{l}}
, we have

\bigg(|\widehat{S}|\big|_{|C_{l}|}\bigg)^{\otimes r}\cong\omega_{|C_{l}|}\otimes{\mathcal{O}}\left(-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
z_{i}\in C_{l}\end{subarray}}a_{i}[z_{i}]-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}i\in I\\
\overline{z_{i}}\in C_{l}\end{subarray}}a_{i}[\overline{z}_{i}]-\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}j\in B\\
x_{j}\in C_{l}\end{subarray}}b_{j}[x_{j}]-\sum_{t\in N_{l}}c_{t}[h_{t}]\right);

note that in the case where
C_{l}
intersects with
\partial\Sigma
, the set
\{h_{t}\}_{t\in N_{l}}
is invariant under
\phi
, and the half-nodes conjugated by
\phi
have the same twist.

Note that if
h_{t_{1}}=\sigma_{0}(h_{t_{2}})
, then

c_{t_{1}}+c_{t_{2}}\equiv-2\mod r.

(2.5)

We say a node is
Ramond
if one (hence both) of its half-nodes
h_{t}
satisfy
c_{t}\equiv-1\mod r
, and it is said to be
Neveu–Schwarz (NS)
otherwise. Note that if a node is Ramond, then both of its half-nodes lie in the set
\mathcal{T}
; moreover, a half-node has twist
-1
if and only if it lies in
\mathcal{A}
. The set
\mathcal{R}
in equation (
2.4
) is chosen in a way that each separating internal Ramond node has precisely
one half-edge in
\mathcal{R}.

Associated to each twisted
r
-spin structure
S
, we define a Serre-dual bundle

J:=S^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{C}.

(2.6)

Note that the involutions on
C
and
L
induce involutions on
S
and
J
; by abuse of notation, we denote the involutions on
S
and
J
also by
\widetilde{\phi}
.

2.1.2
Gradings

For a nodal marked surface, the boundary
{\partial}\Sigma
of
\Sigma
is endowed with a well-defined orientation, determined by the complex orientation on the preferred half
\Sigma\subseteq|C|
. This orientation induces the notion of positivity for
\phi
-invariant sections of
\omega_{|C|}
over
{\partial}\Sigma
: let
p
be a point of
\partial\Sigma
which is not a node, we say a section
s
is
positive
at a
p
if, for any tangent vector
v\in T_{p}({\partial}\Sigma)
in the orientation direction, we have
\langle s(p),v\rangle>0
, where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the natural pairing between cotangent and tangent vectors.

Let
C
be an anchored nodal marked surface, and let
A
be the complement of the special points in
\partial\Sigma
. We say a twisted
r
-spin structure on such
C
is
compatible on the boundary components
if there exists a
\widetilde{\phi}
-invariant section
v\in C^{0}\left(A,|S|^{\widetilde{\phi}}\right)
(called a
lifting
of
S
on boundary components) such that the image of
v^{\otimes r}
under the map on sections induced by the inclusion
|S|^{\otimes r}\to\omega_{|C|}
is positive. We say
w\in C^{0}\left(A,|J|^{\widetilde{\phi}}\right)
is a
Serre-dual lifting of
J
on the boundary components with respect to
v
if
\langle w,v\rangle\in C^{0}(A,\omega_{|C|})
is positive, where
\langle-,-\rangle
is the natural pairing between
|S|^{\vee}
and
|S|
. This
w
is uniquely determined by
v
up to positive rescaling.

Equivalence classes of liftings of
J
(or equivalently,
S
) on the boundary components up to positive rescaling are equivalent to continuous sections of
S^{0}(|J|^{\widetilde{\phi}}),
the
S^{0}
-bundle
\left(|J|^{\widetilde{\phi}}\setminus|J|_{0}\right)\big/\mathbb{R}_{+}
over
A
, where
|J|_{0}
denotes the zero section of
|J|^{\widetilde{\phi}}
. Given an equivalence class
[w]
of liftings, we say a boundary marked point or boundary half-node
x_{j}
is
legal
, or that
[w]

alternates
at
x_{j}
, if
[w]
, as a section of
S^{0}(|J|^{\widetilde{\phi}})
, cannot be continuously extended to
x_{j}
.
We say an equivalence class
[w]
of liftings of
J
on boundaries is a
grading of a twisted
r
-spin structure on boundary components
if, for every Neveu–Schwarz boundary node, one of the two half-nodes is legal and the other is illegal.

Remark 2.4
.

The requirement that every NS boundary node has one legal and one illegal half-node arises from the behaviour of a grading on boundary components at degenerations. This condition, together with (
2.5
) allows smoothing the boundary node. See
[
6
]
for more details.

Let
q
be a contracted boundary node of
C
, we say a twisted
r
-spin structure on
C
is
compatible
at
q
if
q
is Ramond and there exists a
\widetilde{\phi}
-invariant element
v\in|S|\big|_{q}
(called a
lifting
of
S
at
q
) such that the image of
v^{\otimes r}
under the map
|S|^{\otimes r}\big|_{q}\to\omega_{|C|}\big|_{q}
is positive imaginary under the canonical identification of
\omega_{|C|}\big|_{q}
with
{\mathbb{C}}
given by the residue. See
[
6
, Definition 2.8]
for more details. Such a
v
also admits a Serre-dual lifting,
i.e.
a
\widetilde{\phi}
-invariant
w\in|J|\big|_{q}
such that
\langle v,w\rangle
is positive imaginary. We refer to the equivalence classes
[w]
of such
w
up to positive rescaling as a
grading at contracted boundary node
q
.

We say a twisted
r
-spin structure is
compatible
if it is compatible on boundary components and at all contracted boundary nodes. A (total)
grading
is the collection of grading on boundary components together with a grading at each contracted boundary node. We say a grading is
legal
if every boundary marked point is legal.

The grading is crucial in determining a canonical relative orientation for the Witten bundle (in
[
29
]
) and defining canonical boundary conditions (in §
2.3
), which are key ingredients in defining genus-zero open
r
-spin intersection numbers.

The relation between the twists and legality, and the obstructions to having a grading, are summarized in the following proposition.

Proposition 2.5

(
[
4
, Proposition 2.3]
)
.

1.

When
r
is odd, any twisted
r
-spin structure is compatible, and there is a unique grading.

2.

When
r
is odd, a boundary marked point, or boundary half-node,
x_{j}
in a twisted
r
-spin structure with a grading is legal if and only if its twist is odd.

3.

When
r
is even, the boundary twists
b_{j}
in a compatible twisted
r
-spin structure must be even.

4.

In a graded
r
-spin structure, any Neveu--Schwarz boundary node has one legal half-node and one illegal half-node.
3
3

3

This item is part of the definition of grading, we put it here since it is an important property of the grading.

5.

Ramond boundary nodes can appear in a graded
r
-spin structure only when
r
is odd, and in this case, their half-nodes are illegal with twists
r-1.

6.

For a twisted
r
-spin structure over a connected marked disk, there exists grading that alternates precisely at a subset
D\subset\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B}
if and only if

\frac{2\sum a_{i}+\sum b_{j}+2}{r}\equiv|D|\mod 2.

(2.7)

7.

For a twisted
r
-spin structure over a connected marked cylinder with two boundary components
\partial_{1}\Sigma
and
\partial_{2}\Sigma
, there exists grading that alternates precisely at a subset
D\subset\{x_{j}\}_{j\in B}
if and only if

|D\cap\partial_{i}\Sigma|\equiv\Theta_{i}\mod 2

(2.8)

for
i=1,2
, where
\Theta_{i}:=0
if
|J|^{\tilde{\phi}}_{\partial_{i}\Sigma}
is orientable and
\Theta_{i}:=1
if
|J|^{\tilde{\phi}}_{\partial_{i}\Sigma}
is not orientable.
In particular, we have

\frac{2\sum a_{i}+\sum b_{j}}{r}\equiv|D|\mod 2.

(2.9)

When a Ramond contracted boundary node is normalized, the grading at this boundary node induces an additional structure at the corresponding half-node. We call such half-nodes by
normalized contracted boundary marked point
. Because such an additional structure is not necessary in this paper, we refer the readers to
[
29
, Definition 2.5]
for the precise definition.

We can now define the primary objects of interest in this paper:

Definition 2.6
.

A
stable graded
r
-spin disk or cylinder
(respectively,
legal stable graded
r
-spin disk or cylinder
) is a stable anchored nodal marked disk or cylinder, together with

1.

a compatible twisted
r
-spin structure
S
in which all contracted boundary nodes are Ramond;

2.

a choice of grading (respectively, legal gradings such that all boundary marking have twist
r-2
);

3.

an additional structure of normalized contracted boundary marked point at a subset of markings with twist
r-1
.

Remark 2.7
.

The “legal stable graded
r
-spin disk” here is referred to as “legal stable graded level-
0

r
-spin disk” in
[
29
,
30
]
. In this paper we will focus on the case where all the legal markings have twist
r-2
as in
[
4
]
. We will omit the term “level-
0
”.

We denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
(
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
respectively) the moduli space of connected stable graded
r
-spin surfaces (legal connected stable graded
r
-spin surfaces respectively) of genus
g
with
k
boundary and
l
internal marked points. In
[
6
, Theorem 3.4]
and
[
29
, Theorem 2.8]
,
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
are shown to be a compact smooth orientable orbifold with corners, of real dimension

\dim_{\mathbb{R}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}=k+2l+3g-3.

(2.10)

Notation 2.8
.

Assuming that the internal marked points
\{z_{i}\}_{i\in I}
have twists
\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, by an abuse of notation, we also denote the multiset
4
4

4

We use the word “multiset” here because the set
I
may contain multiple
a_{i}
with a same value, but we view them as different elements.

\{a_{i}\}_{i\in I}
by
I
.

Similarly, we denote by
B
the multiset
\{b_{j}\}_{j\in B}
equipped with a preselected legality for each of its elements. Furthermore, if
B
is equipped with a cyclic order on
\sigma\colon B\to B
, we denote it by
\bar{B}
and write
\bar{B}=\overline{\{b_{1},b_{2},\dots,b_{\lvert B\rvert}\}}
to make the cyclic order manifest, where
\sigma_{2}(b_{i})=b_{i+1}
for
1\leq i\leq\lvert B\rvert
and
\sigma_{2}(b_{\lvert B\rvert})=b_{1}
.

We denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{0,B,I}^{1/r}
the moduli space of graded
r
-spin disks with boundary points marked by
B
, and internal points marked by
I
. Note that for a graded
r
-spin disk, the canonical orientation on
\partial\Sigma
induces a cyclic order on
B
; given a cyclic order
\sigma_{2}\colon B\to B
, we denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{0,\bar{B},I}^{1/r}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{0,{B},I}^{1/r}
the connected component parametrizing the
r
-spin disks such that the induced cyclic order on
B
coincides with
\sigma_{2}
.
Similarly, We denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,B,I}^{1/r}
the moduli space of graded
r
-spin cylinders with boundary points marked by
B
, and internal points marked by
I
. Note that for a graded
r
-spin cylinder with two boundary components
\partial_{i}\Sigma
where
i=1,2
, the canonical orientation on
\partial_{i}\Sigma
induces a cyclic order on
B^{i}
, where
B^{i}\subset B
is the markings corresponding to marked points on
\partial_{i}\Sigma
; given a partition
B=B^{1}\sqcup B^{2}
, and a cyclic order
\sigma_{2}^{i}\colon B^{i}\to B^{i}
for each
B^{i}
, we denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,\{\bar{B}^{1},\bar{B}^{2}\},I}^{1/r}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,{B},I}^{1/r}
the connected component parametrizing the
r
-spin cylinders such that the induced cyclic order on
B^{i}
coincides with
\sigma^{i}_{2}
.

Remark 2.9
.

In most parts of this article, we primarily consider moduli of legal graded
r
-spin disks of cylinders. We use the notation with a superscript
*
only when we want to emphasize that there might be illegal boundary markings.

2.1.3
Stable graded
r
-spin graphs

Each connected anchored marked surface
\Sigma
can be characterized by a decorated dual graph
\Gamma(\Sigma)
as follow.

•

The set of vertices of
\Gamma(\Sigma)
is the set of irreducible components of
\Sigma
, which is decomposed into
open
and
closed
vertices
V=V^{O}\sqcup V^{C}
5
5

5

Since
V,V^{O}
or
V^{C}
depend on the graph
\Gamma(\Sigma)
, we should write them as
V(\Gamma(\Sigma)),V^{O}(\Gamma(\Sigma))
or
V^{C}(\Gamma(\Sigma))
to be precise. We omit
\Gamma(\Sigma)
from the notation here (and in following items) for simplicity because there is no no ambiguity.
depending on whether the corresponding irreducible component meets
\partial\Sigma
.
Each vertex has a genus: the genus of the irreducible component.

•

The set of half-edges
H(v)
emanating from a vertex
v\in V
is the set of the special points (
i.e.
half-nodes and marked points) on the irreducible component corresponding to
v
. The set
H(v)
is decomposed into
boundary
and
internal
half-edges
H(v)=H^{B}(v)\sqcup H^{I}(v)
depending on whether the corresponding special point lies on
\partial\Sigma
. We write
H:=\sqcup_{v\in V}H(v)
and
H=H^{B}\sqcup H^{I}
the correspond decomposition. Two half-edges correspond to an (internal or boundary, separating or non-separating) edge
e
in the set of edges

E=E^{I}\sqcup E^{B}=E_{sp}\sqcup E_{nsp}=E^{I}_{sp}\sqcup E^{I}_{nsp}\sqcup E^{B}_{sp}\sqcup E^{B}_{nsp}

if their corresponding special points are two half-nodes of the same (internal or boundary, separating or non-separating) node. We denote by
H^{I},H^{B},H_{sp},H_{nsp},H^{I}_{sp},H^{I}_{nsp},H^{B}_{sp},H^{B}_{nsp}
the corresponding sets of half-edges.

•

When
\Sigma
is a cylinder, as mentioned in Remark
2.2
, for each genus-zero
v\in V
, there are one or two special boundary half-edge(s) in
RT(v)\subseteq H^{B}(v)
corresponding to the root(s).

•

The set of
tails

T
is the set of all marked points together with the contracted boundary nodes. We write
T^{B}:=T\cap H^{B}
and
T^{I}:=T\cap H^{I}
. The set of
contracted boundary tails

H^{CB}\subseteq T^{I}
corresponds to the contracted boundary node and the set
T^{anc}\subseteq T^{I}\setminus H^{CB}
corresponds to the anchor.

•

The canonical orientation on
\partial\Sigma
induced a map
\sigma_{2}\colon H^{B}(v)\to H^{B}(v)
for each
v\in V^{O}
, which can be viewed as a cyclic order on each of its orbits.

We say
\Gamma(\Sigma)
is
smooth
if
E=H^{CB}=\emptyset
, or equivalently,
\Sigma
is smooth.
If
\Sigma
is endowed with a graded
r
-spin structure
S
, we have the additional decorations:

•

a map
\text{tw}:H\rightarrow\{-1,0,1,\ldots,r-1\}
encoding the twist of
S
at each special points;

•

a map

\text{alt}:H^{B}\rightarrow\mathbb{Z}/2\mathbb{Z}

given by
\text{alt}(h)=1
if the special point corresponding to
h
is legal and
\text{alt}(h)=0
otherwise.

A
genus-zero (respectively, genus-one) graded
r
-spin graph
is a decorated graph for which each connected component is the dual graph of a connected graded
r
-spin disk (respectively, cylinder) as above; an intrinsic definition can be found in
[
29
, §3.2]
. A graph
\Gamma
is
stable
if
\lvert H^{B}(v)\rvert+2\lvert H^{I}(B)(v)\geq 3
for each genus-zero
v\in V(\Gamma)
and
\lvert H^{B}(v)\rvert+2\lvert H^{I}(B)(v)\geq 1
for each genus-one
v\in V(\Gamma)
.

For an edge
e
(or a set of edges
S
) of a stable graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma=\Gamma(\Sigma)
, the
smoothing
of
\Gamma
along
e
(or
S
) is the stable graded
r
-spin graph
d_{e}\Gamma
(or
d_{S}\Gamma
) that is dual to the stable graded
r
-spin surface obtained by smoothing the node(s) in
\Sigma
corresponding to
e
(or
S
). The
detaching
of
\Gamma
along
e
(or
S
) is the stable graded
r
-spin graph
\text{Detach}_{e}\Gamma
(or
\text{Detach}_{S}\Gamma
) that is dual to the stable graded
r
-spin surface obtained by normalizing the node(s) in
\Sigma
corresponding to
e
(or
S
). See
[
29
, §3.2]
for intrinsic definitions. We set

\displaystyle\partial^{!}\Gamma=\{\Lambda\;\colon\;\Lambda\text{ stable, }\Gamma=d_{S}\Lambda\text{ for some }S\subset E(\Lambda)\},

\displaystyle\partial\Gamma=\partial^{!}\Gamma\setminus\{\Gamma\},

\displaystyle\partial^{B}\Gamma=\{\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma\;\colon\;E^{B}(\Lambda)\cup H^{CB}(\Lambda)\neq\emptyset\}.

We say a stable graded
r
-spin graph is
legal
if every boundary tail is legal,
i.e.

{\text{alt}}(t)=1~\forall t\in T^{B}
, and of twist
r-2
(which is referred to as legal level-
0
graded
r
-spin graph in
[
4
]
).

If
\Gamma
is a connected stable graded
r
-spin graph, we denote by
{\mathcal{M}^{*}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}\subseteq{{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}}_{g,T^{B},T^{I}\setminus H^{CB},}^{1/r}
the submoduli consisting of
r
-spin surfaces whose dual graph is exactly
\Gamma
, and by
\overline{{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}
its closure. If
\Gamma
is disconnected, we define
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}
as the product of the moduli spaces associated to its connected components. When there is no room for confusion (which is always the case, except in §
2.2.3
), we omit the superscript
1/r
in
\overline{{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}
.
As the twist and legality of the boundary half-edges (
i.e.
the map
tw
and
alt
) are parts of data of
\Gamma
, we will also omit the superscript
*
in the notation when there is no ambiguity.
If
\Gamma
is single-vertex and unstable, we formally take
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}^{1/r}_{\Gamma}
to be a single point.

For a stable dual graph
\Gamma
and a subset
S\subseteq T^{I}(\Gamma)
of tails, we denote by
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
the dual graph obtained by forgetting all the tails in
S
. When
\Gamma
is a stable graded
r
-spin graph and all the tails in
S
are either twist-zero internal tail or twist-zero illegal boundary tail,
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is also a graded
r
-spin graph. Note that
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is not necessary stable, we denote by
\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)
the graph obtained by
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
after contracting all the unstable vertices;
\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)
is either stable, or single-vertex unstable. We denote by

\operatorname{For_{\Gamma\to\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)}

or

\operatorname{For_{\Gamma\to\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}^{1/r}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}^{1/r}_{\overline{\operatorname{for}}_{S}(\Gamma)}

the corresponding forgetful morphism.

2.2
The Witten bundle and the relative cotangent line bundles

2.2.1
Genus-zero Witten bundle

The
Witten bundle
is the protagonist of the
r
-spin theory. Roughly speaking, let
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{0,k,l}^{1/r}
be the universal curve and
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}
be the twisted universal spin bundle with the universal Serre-dual bundle

\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{S}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\pi},

(2.11)

then we define a sheaf

{\mathcal{W}}:=(R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{J})_{+},

(2.12)

where the subscript
+
denotes invariant sections under the universal involution
\widetilde{\phi}:\mathcal{J}\to\mathcal{J}.

To be more precise, defining
{\mathcal{W}}
by (
2.12
) would require dealing with derived pushforward in the category of orbifold-with-corners. To avoid this technicality, we define
{\mathcal{W}}
by pullback of the analogous sheaf from a subset of the closed moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,k+2l}^{1/r}
; see
[
6
, §4.1]
.

In this
g=0
case,
\mathcal{W}
is in fact a vector bundle, this follows from a direct Riemann–Roch computation showing that

R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}=0.

(2.13)

On a non-empty component of the moduli space which parametrizes
r
-spin disks with internal twists
\{a_{i}\}
and boundary twists
\{b_{j}\}
, the (real) rank of the Witten bundle is

\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}-(r-2)}{r}.

(2.14)

2.2.2
Genus-one Witten bundle

In the
g=1
case, we still have the universal curve
\pi:\mathcal{C}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,k,l}^{1/r}
and the twisted universal spin bundle
\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{C}
with the universal Serre-dual bundle

\mathcal{J}:=\mathcal{S}^{\vee}\otimes\omega_{\pi}

as in
g=0
case. However,
(
2.13
) is not true in this case so
\mathcal{W}
is not a vector bundle over the entire moduli. What we do is to remove the “dimension-jump locus”
\mathcal{Z}^{dj}
,
i.e.,
the support of
R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}
, from the
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,k,l}^{1/r}
, and define

{\mathcal{W}}:=(R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{J})_{+},

as a vector bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{1,k,l}^{1/r}\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{dj}
.

Definition 2.10
.

For a
r
-spin (nodal) cylinder
\Sigma
, we say
\Sigma
is
dimension-jump
if there exists
\Sigma^{\prime}\subseteq\Sigma
such that

•

\Sigma^{\prime}
is a union of irreducible components of
\Sigma
;

•

\Sigma^{\prime}
is genus-one;

•

the restriction of
S
to
\Sigma^{\prime}
is a trivial line bundle.

Remark 2.11
.

Note that
S|_{\Sigma^{\prime}}
is trivial implies that all the internal and boundary markings on
\Sigma^{\prime}
have twist
0
. Moreover, all the half-nodes on
\Sigma^{\prime}
connecting
\Sigma^{\prime}
to
\Sigma\setminus\Sigma^{\prime}
have twist
0
.

Definition 2.12
.

We define the dimension-jump loci inside
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{1,k,l}
to be the

\mathcal{Z}^{dj}:=\left\{[\Sigma]\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{1,k,l}\colon\Sigma\text{ is dimension-jump}\right\}.

We write

{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{1,k,l}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{1,k,l}\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{dj};

for a
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
, we also write

\mathcal{Z}^{dj}_{\Gamma}:=\mathcal{Z}^{dj}\cap{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\quad{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\mathcal{Z}^{dj}_{\Gamma}.

Remark 2.13
.

Note that all non-separating boundary nodes for
\Sigma
in
\mathcal{Z}^{dj}
are Ramond nodes (whose half-nodes have twist
r-1
), therefore for any
\Gamma
with a non-separating NS edge with have
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
.
Moreover, when
r
is even, by item
5
of Proposition
2.5
, Ramond boundary nodes can not appear; therefore, we have
\mathcal{Z}^{dj}=\emptyset
when
r
is even.

Example 2.14
.

When
r
is odd, let
\Delta
be a smooth
r
-spin graph with a single genus-one vertex,
k
boundary and
l
internal markings with twist zero. Then topologically the moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}
is the disjoint union of
r
copies
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,k,l}
and the dimension-jump loci
\mathcal{Z}^{dj}\cap{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}
is one of these
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,k,l}
. Therefore in this case
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Delta}
is the disjoint union of
r-1
copies
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,k,l}
.

We define the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{1,k,l}
as

{\mathcal{W}}:=(R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{J})_{+}.

On a non-empty component of the moduli space which parametrizes
r
-spin cylinders with internal twists
\{a_{i}\}
and boundary twists
\{b_{j}\}
, the (real) rank of the Witten bundle is

\frac{2\sum_{i\in I}a_{i}+\sum_{j\in B}b_{j}}{r}.

(2.15)

For genus-zero
\Gamma
, we will formally write
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
.
In
[
29
, §3.2]
, a canonical relative orientation
o_{\Gamma}
of the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
is defined for every connected genus-zero or genus-one legal stable graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
.

2.2.3
Decomposition properties of the Witten bundle

In
[
6
,
29
]
the Witten bundle is proven to satisfy certain decomposition properties along nodes. We state these properties here, further details and proofs can be found in
[
29
]
and
[
6
, §4.2]
.

Given a stable graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
of genus-
g
for
g=0
or
1
, let
\widehat{\Gamma}
be obtained by detaching either an edge or a contracted boundary tail of
\Gamma
. In order to state the decomposition properties of the Witten bundle, we need the morphisms

{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{1/r}\xleftarrow{q}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\times_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}\xrightarrow{\mu}{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}\xrightarrow{i_{\Gamma}}\overline{\mathcal{QM}^{*}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r},

(2.16)

where
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}
is the moduli space of marked surfaces (without
r
-spin structure) corresponding to the dual graph
\Gamma
. The morphism
q
is defined by sending the
r
-spin structure
S
to the
r
-spin structure
\widehat{S}
defined by (
2.4
); it has degree one but is not an isomorphism because it does not induce an isomorphism between isotropy groups. The morphism
\mu
is the projection to the second factor in the fibre product; it is a surjective morphism, and is an isomorphism when
\Gamma
has no non-separating edges. The morphism
i_{\Gamma}
is the inclusion.

We denote by
{\mathcal{W}}
and
\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}
the Witten bundles on
\overline{\mathcal{QM}^{*}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{1/r}
, the decomposition properties below show how these bundles are related under pullback via the morphisms (
2.16
).

Proposition 2.15

(
[
29
, Proposition 2.20]
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus-zero or genus-one stable graded
r
-spin graph with a single edge
e
, and let
\widehat{\Gamma}
be the detaching of
\Gamma
along
e
. Then the Witten bundle decomposes as follows:

1.

If
e
is a Neveu–Schwarz edge, then

\mu^{*}i_{\Gamma}^{*}{\mathcal{W}}=q^{*}\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}.

(2.17)

2.

If
e
is a Ramond boundary edge, then there is an exact sequence

0\to\mu^{*}i_{\Gamma}^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to q^{*}\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}\to\underline{\mathbb{R}_{+}}\to 0,

(2.18)

where
\underline{\mathbb{R}_{+}}
is a trivial real line bundle.

3.

If
e
is a Ramond internal edge connecting two closed genus-zero vertices, write
q^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\boxplus\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is the Witten bundle on the component containing a contracted boundary tail or the anchor of
\Gamma
, and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
is the Witten bundle on the other component. Then there is an exact sequence

0\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}\to\mu^{*}i_{\Gamma}^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\to 0.

(2.19)

Furthermore, if
\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime}
is defined to agree with
\widehat{\Gamma}
except that the twist at each Ramond tail is
r-1
, and
q^{\prime}:{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}\times_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime}}^{1/r}
is defined analogously to
q
, then there is an exact sequence

0\to\mu^{*}i_{\Gamma}^{*}{\mathcal{W}}\to(q^{\prime})^{*}\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}\to{\underline{\mathbb{C}}}^{1/r}\to 0,

(2.20)

where
\widehat{{\mathcal{W}}}^{\prime}
is the Witten bundle on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}^{\prime}}^{1/r}
and
{\underline{\mathbb{C}}}^{1/r}
is a line bundle whose
r
-th power is trivial.

4.

If
e
is a separating Ramond internal edge connecting an open vertex to a closed vertex, write
q^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\boxplus\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
is the Witten bundle on the open component (defined via
\widehat{\mathcal{S}}|_{\mathcal{C}_{1}}
) and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
is the Witten bundle on the closed component. Then the exact sequences (
2.19
) and (
2.20
) both hold.

Analogously, for genus-zero
\Gamma
which has a single vertex, no edges, and a contracted boundary tail
t
, and
\widehat{\Gamma}
is the detaching of
\Gamma
along
t
, then there is a decomposition property:

5.

If
{\mathcal{W}}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}
denote the Witten bundles on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{0,k,l}^{1/r}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{1/r}
, respectively, then the sequence (
2.18
) holds.

Remark 2.16
.

If the edge
e
is a Neveu–Schwarz boundary edge, then the map
q
is an isomorphism, and in this case, the proposition implies that the Witten bundle pulls back under the gluing morphism
\mu\circ q^{-1}\colon\overline{\mathcal{QM}^{*}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}}^{1/r}\to\overline{\mathcal{QM}^{*}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
.

Notation 2.17
.

Assuming
\Gamma
has only NS boundary edges and
\widehat{\Gamma}
has
t
connected components
\Delta_{1},\dots,\Delta_{t}
. If we denote by
\mathcal{W}_{\Delta_{i}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Delta_{i}}
the corresponding Witten bundles then we have
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}=\bboxplus_{i=1}^{t}\mathcal{W}_{\Delta_{i}}
. For a multisection
s_{\Gamma}
of
\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}=i_{\Gamma}^{*}\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
and multisections
s_{\Delta_{i}}
of
\mathcal{W}_{\Delta_{i}}
,
by abuse of notation, we will write

s_{\Gamma}=\bboxplus_{i=1}^{t}s_{\Delta_{i}}

as long as

\mu^{*}s_{\Gamma}=q^{*}\left(\bboxplus_{i=1}^{t}s_{\Delta_{i}}\right).

2.2.4
Coherent sections and the assembling operator

With the same notation as in Proposition
2.15
, let
\Gamma
be a genus-zero or genus-one connected graded
r
-spin graph and
e
be a separating edge of
\Gamma
, let
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{2}}
be the two components of
\widehat{\Gamma}:={\text{detach}}_{e}{\Gamma}
. We write
q^{*}\widehat{\mathcal{W}}=\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}\boxplus\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
, where
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
are the (pullback of) Witten bundles on
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{1}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\widehat{\Gamma}_{2}}
.
Given sections
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
of
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1}
and
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2}
, we want to construct a section of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
. In the case where
e
is an NS boundary edge, according to Remark
2.16
, we can glue
s_{1},s_{2}

to a section of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
. However, when
e
is an internal edge, we cannot glue
s_{1}
and
s_{2}
directly. In the case of an internal NS edge, this is because the automorphism groups of
{\mathcal{W}}
and of the direct sum are not the same. Ramond internal edges introduce a more fundamental problem, since
{\mathcal{W}}
does not decompose naturally as a direct sum of the
\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{1},\widehat{\mathcal{W}}_{2},
by Proposition
2.15
above. To construct a section of
\mathcal{W}\to\overline{\mathcal{QM}}_{\Gamma}
, we need the
assembling operator
introduced in
[
4
]
, which is based on the following technical notion of
coherent multisections
.

Let
\Gamma_{c}
be a genus-zero connected stable
r
-spin dual graph with an anchor
t\in T^{I}(\Gamma_{c})
,
i.e.

\Gamma_{c}
has no open vertices or contracted boundary tails.

•

If
\text{tw}(t)=-1
, let
\mathcal{J}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
be the universal Serre-dual bundle as in (
2.11
), we set
\mathcal{J}^{\prime}:=\mathcal{J}\otimes\mathcal{O}\left(r\Delta_{z_{t}}\right)
, where
\Delta_{z_{t}}
is the divisor in the universal curve corresponding to the anchor
t
.
We define an orbifold bundle
{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
by

{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}:=\sigma_{z_{t}}^{*}\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
,
where
\sigma_{z_{t}}
is the section corresponding to the anchor
t
in the universal curve. We also abuse notations to denote by
{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
the total space of this bundle. We write

\wp:{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
for the projection. We denote by
{\mathcal{W}}
the bundle
R^{0}\pi_{*}\mathcal{J}^{\prime}
on
{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
.

•

If
\text{tw}(t)\neq-1
, we set
{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
and set
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
the same as
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
.

Definition 2.18

(
[
4
, Definition 4.2]
)
.

Let
\Gamma_{c}
be a connected genus-zero stable
r
-spin dual graph with an anchor
t\in T^{I}(\Gamma_{c})
, and let
s
be a section of
\mathcal{W}
over a subset
U\subset{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
. We say
s
is
coherent
if either the twist
\text{tw}(t)\neq-1
, or, in the case where
\text{tw}(t)=-1
, for any point
\zeta=(C,u_{t})\in U
corresponding to a graded
r
-spin disk
C
and an element
u_{t}
in the fibre over
z_{t}\in C
of the line bundle
J^{\prime}:=J\otimes\mathcal{O}(r[z_{t}])\to C
, the element
s(\zeta)\in H^{0}(J^{\prime})
satisfies

\operatorname{ev}_{z_{t}}s(\zeta)=u_{t}.

A coherent multisection
s
is defined as a multisection (see
[
4
, Appendix A]
) whose local branches are coherent.

Let
s
be a coherent multisection of
\mathcal{W}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
. Note that, in the case
\text{tw}(t)=-1
, for any
\zeta\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}\hookrightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
, the evaluation
\operatorname{ev}_{z_{t}}(s(\zeta))
is equal to zero, thus
s(\zeta)
is induced by a multisection of
J
. In other words, the restriction of a coherent multisection
s
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
is canonically identified with a multisection of
{\mathcal{W}}\rightarrow{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
; we denote this induced multisection by
\overline{s}
. In case
\text{tw}(t)\neq-1
, we write
\overline{s}=s
.

Lemma 2.19

(
[
30
, Lemma 2.12]
)
.

Let
\Gamma_{c}
be a connected graded
r
-spin graph with an anchor
t
, for any multisection
\hat{s}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
,
there exist a coherent multisection
s
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
satisfying
\overline{s}=\hat{s}
.

Given a connected graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
with an open vertex or a contracted boundary, let
e
be a separating internal edge
e\in E_{nsp}^{I}(\Gamma)
. We denote by
\Gamma_{o}
and
\Gamma_{c}
the connected components of
{\text{detach}}_{e}\Gamma
, where
\Gamma_{o}
has an open vertex or a contracted boundary tail (hence
\Gamma_{c}
has an anchor). Using the assembling operator
{{\,\maltese\,}}
defined in
[
4
, §4.1.3]
, we can glue a multisection
s_{o}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma_{o}}
and a coherent multisection
s_{c}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{R}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
to obtain a multisection
s_{o}{{\,\maltese\,}}s_{c}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
. By construction the multisection
s_{o}{{\,\maltese\,}}s_{c}
satisfies the following property.

Lemma 2.20
.

With the above notation, let
\pi_{o}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma_{o}}
and
\pi_{c}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
be the projections.
For any multisection
s_{o}
of
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma_{o}}
, and a coherent multisection
s_{c}
of
\mathcal{W}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{\Gamma_{c}}
, the multisection
s_{o}{{\,\maltese\,}}s_{c}
vanishes at
p\in{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
if and only
\overline{s_{c}}(\pi_{c}(p))=0
and
s_{o}(\pi_{o}(p))=0
.

We refer the reader to
[
4
, §4.1.3]
for further details and exact definitions.

2.2.5
Relative cotangent line bundles

Other important line bundles in open
r
-spin theory are the
relative cotangent line bundles
or
tautological line bundles
at internal marked points. These line bundles have already been defined on the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}
of stable marked surfaces (without spin structure) in
[
23
]
, as the line bundles with fibre
T^{*}_{z_{i}}\Sigma
. Equivalently, these line bundles are the pullback of the usual relative cotangent line bundles
{\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k+2l}
under the doubling map
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k+2l}
that sends
\Sigma
to
C=\Sigma\sqcup_{\partial\Sigma}\overline{\Sigma}
. The bundle
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}^{*}}}_{g,k,l}^{1/r}
is the pullback of this relative cotangent line bundle on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{g,k,l}
under the morphism
\text{For}_{\text{spin}}
that forgets the spin structure. Note that
\mathbb{L}_{i}
is a complex line bundle, hence it carries a canonical orientation. Further discussion can be found in §
3
.

2.3
Positivity boundary condition for sections of genus-zero Witten bundle

In the construction of genus-zero
r
-spin theory
[
4
,
30
]
, an important concept is the positivity boundary condition for the sections of the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}
.

Let
\Gamma
be a graded (genus-zero or genus-one)
r
-spin graph, we refer to a boundary edge
e\in E^{B}(\Gamma),
or the corresponding node, as
positive
if one half-edge
h_{1}
of
e
satisfies
{\text{alt}}(h_{1})=0
and
\text{tw}(h_{1})>0
. For a graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
we write

H^{+}(\Gamma):=\left\{h\in H^{B}(\Gamma)\colon\text{either $h$ or $\sigma_{1}(h)$ is positive}\right\},

\partial^{+}\Gamma:=\{{\Delta}\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\colon H^{+}({\Delta})\neq\emptyset\}.

\partial^{CB}\Gamma:=\{{\Delta}\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\colon H^{CB}({\Delta})\neq\emptyset\}

and

\partial^{*}\Gamma:=\{{\Delta}\in\partial^{!}\Gamma\colon H^{I}_{nsp}=H^{+}({\Delta})=\emptyset\}

For a graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
, we define

\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}:=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{+}\Gamma}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda},\quad{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}:={\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}\setminus\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma},\quad\partial^{CB}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}:=\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\partial^{CB}\Gamma}{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}

and similarly
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{1/r}_{g,B,I}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{g,B,I}
,
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{1/r}_{g,k,l}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{g,k,l}
as disjoint unions over smooth
\Gamma
.

Definition 2.21
.

Let
C
be a graded
r
-spin surface,
q\in C
a point, and
v\in{\mathcal{W}}_{C}
. The
evaluation
of
v
at
q
is
\text{ev}_{q}(v):=v(q)\in J_{q}
. In particular, if
q
is a contracted boundary node, or a point on
\Sigma
which is not a legal special point, we say
v

evaluates positively
at
q
if
\text{ev}_{q}(v)
is positive with respect to the grading.

Roughly speaking, for genus-zero
\Gamma
a positive multisection of the
\mathcal{W}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
is a multisection that satisfies certain positivity constraints at
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\cup\partial^{CB}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
. The positivity constraint at a
\partial^{CB}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
for a multisection
s
is chosen to be that the evaluation of
s
at the contracted boundary node is positive. A naïve definition of the positivity constraint at
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
for a multisection
s
is that the evaluation of
s
at the illegal half-node is positive; however, this constraint is too strong and the canonical multisection may not exist (see
[
4
, Example 3.23]
).

As in
[
4
]
, instead of imposing
positivity constraints at
