\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, we impose positive constraints at their neighbourhoods (therefore we work on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
) and the positive evaluations will be required on certain “intervals” in the boundary of disk, which we now recall; they should be viewed as a smoothly-varying family of intervals that approximate neighbourhoods of boundary nodes in a nodal disk.

Remark 2.22
.

An alternative, equivalent, way for defining the boundary conditions but working on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
rather than
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is to allow vanishing of sections of the Witten bundle on the boundary, but requiring a Neumann-like boundary condition on the derivative, following
[
4
]

we chose to work on
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}.

Definition 2.23

(
[
4
, Definition 3.4]
)
.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus-zero graded
r
-spin graph and let
\Lambda\in\partial\Gamma
.
We say an open set
U\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
is a
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
if
U
does not intersect with the strata
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Xi}
for any
\Xi\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
satisfying
\Lambda\notin\partial^{!}\Xi
.
We say a neighbourhood
U\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
of
u\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
is a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood of
u
with respect to
\Gamma
if it is a
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
. Since there is a unique smooth graph
\Gamma
with
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Gamma
, we refer to a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood with respect to a smooth
\Gamma
simply as a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood.

Let
\Sigma
be the preferred half of a graded marked disk. We write
\partial\Sigma=S/\sim
for a space
S
homeomorphic to
S^{1}
and denote by
q\colon S\to\partial\Sigma
the quotient map. An
interval

I
is the image of a connected open set of
S
under
q
. Note that the preimage of
I
under the quotient map
q
is the union of an open set with a finite number of isolated points.

Suppose
C\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Lambda}
, and let
n_{h}
be a boundary half-node corresponding to a half-edge
h\in H^{B}(\Lambda)
. We denote by
N:\widehat{C}\to C
the normalization map and write
\widehat{\Sigma}=N^{-1}(\Sigma)
, We say that
n_{h}

belongs
to the interval
I
if the following two conditions hold:

•

the corresponding node
N(n_{h})
lies in
I
;

•

N^{-1}(I)
contains a half-open interval with starting point
n_{h}
and a half-open interval with endpoint
n_{\sigma_{1}(h)}
, where the starting and end points are determined by the canonical orientation of
\partial\widehat{\Sigma}
.

Let
U
be a
\Lambda
-set. We denote by
\pi:\lvert\mathcal{C}\rvert\to U
the coarse universal curve and by
\Sigma_{u}\subseteq\pi^{-1}(u)
the preferred half in the fibre. A
\Lambda
-family of intervals

\{I_{h}(u)\}_{h\in{H^{+}}(\Lambda),u\in U}
for
U
is a choice of an interval
I_{h}(u)
for each
h\in{H^{+}}(\Lambda)
and
u\in U
, such that:

1.

The endpoints of each
I_{h}(u)
vary smoothly with respect to the smooth structure of the universal curve restricted to
U
.

We say that
\Xi
is
a smoothing of
\Lambda
away from
h\in H^{+}(\Lambda)
if
\Lambda\in\partial^{!}\Xi
and
h
lies in the image of the injection
\iota:H^{+}(\Xi)\to H^{+}(\Lambda)
. We further require that:

2.

If
h\notin\{h^{\prime},\sigma_{1}(h^{\prime})\}
, we have
I_{h}(u^{\prime})\cap I_{h^{\prime}}(u^{\prime})=\emptyset
. If
h=\sigma_{1}(h^{\prime})
, we have
I_{h}(u^{\prime})\cap I_{h^{\prime}}(u^{\prime})\neq\emptyset
if and only if
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Xi}
for some smoothing
\Xi
of
\Lambda
away from
h
.
In this case,
I_{h}(u^{\prime})\cap I_{\sigma_{1}(h)}(u^{\prime})
consists exactly of the node
N(n_{h})
.

3.

There are no marked points in
I_{h}(u^{\prime})
. the interval
I_{h}(u^{\prime})
contains at most one half-node; it contains one half-node if and only if
u^{\prime}\in{\mathcal{M}}_{\Xi}
for some smoothing
\Xi
of
\Lambda
away from
h
. The half-node that belongs to
I_{h}(u^{\prime})
is
n_{h}
in this case.

If the moduli point
u
represents the stable graded disk
C
we write
I_{h}(C)
for
I_{h}(u).
Figure
2
shows the local picture of the intervals in the nodal and smooth disks.

:

I_{h}
and

associated to the half-nodes

and

are drawn over the thinner boundary lines. The image on the right represents a point in the moduli space that is close to the image on the left, where the node is smoothed.

Definition 2.24
.

Let
C
be a graded
r
-spin disk, and let
A\subseteq\partial\Sigma
be a subset without legal special points. Then an element
w\in{\mathcal{W}}_{\Sigma}

evaluates positively at
A
if
\text{ev}_{x}(w)
is positive for every
x\in A
with respect to the grading.

Let
\Gamma
be a genus-zero graded graded graph,
U
a
\Lambda
-set with respect to
\Gamma
, and
\{I_{h}\}
a
\Lambda
-family of intervals for
U
. Given a multisection
s
of
{\mathcal{W}}
defined in a subset of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
containing
U\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, we say
s
is
(U,I)
-positive
(with respect to
\Gamma
) if for any
u^{\prime}\in U\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, any local branch
s_{i}(u^{\prime})
evaluates positively at each
I_{h}(u^{\prime})
.

We say a multisection
s
defined in
W\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
, where
W
is a neighbourhood of
u\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda}
, is
positive near

u
(with respect to
\Gamma
) if there exists a
\Lambda
-neighbourhood
U\subseteq W
of
u
and a
\Lambda
-family of intervals
I_{*}(-)
for
U
such that
s
is
(U,I)
-positive.

If
W
is a neighbourhood of
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, then a multisection
s
defined in a set

U_{+,\Gamma}=\left(W\cup\partial^{CB}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\right)\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}

(2.21)

is
positive
(with respect to
\Gamma
) if it is positive near each point of
\partial^{+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
and evaluates positively at the contracted boundary nodes. As above, we omit the phrase “with respect to
\Gamma
” if
\Gamma
is smooth.

For a genus-zero
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
, there always exist positive multisections of
\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
according to
[
4
, Proposition 3.20]
. However, for genus-one
\Gamma
, although we can define similar positivity condition for multisections of Witten bundle, the positive multisection might not exist.

2.4
The point insertion technique

Just like the closed
r
-spin theory considers an intersection theory over the moduli spaces of
r
-spin curves, the (genus-zero or genus-one) open
r
-spin theory considers the intersection theory over the moduli of the
r
-spin surfaces
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{g,B,I}
. However, since
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{g,B,I}
is a orbifold with corners, the intersection theory is not well-defined. The grading structure allows us to deal with certain type of boundaries using a notion of positivity (see §
2.3
). The procedure of
point insertion
, which also relies on the grading, was developed in
[
29
]
in order to treat the remaining boundaries: we can glue another moduli spaces to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{g,B,I}
along those strata and by that cancel them.

*
\begin{subarray}{c}2h\\

\text{illegal}\end{subarray}

r-2-2h\end{subarray}
v_{1}
\cong
*
h
\begin{subarray}{c}2h\\
\text{illegal}\end{subarray}
v_{2}

*
\begin{subarray}{c}\text{legal}\\

\cong
v_{2}

\supset
as a boundary

\subset

Figure 3
:
In point insertion procedure we glue
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
together along their isomorphic boundaries
\text{bd}_{BI}
and
\text{bd}_{AI}
. The first isomorphism follows from the decomposition property for boundary NS nodes; the second isomorphism holds because the moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{0.\{r-2-2h\},\{h\}}
(represented by the smallest bubble in the figure) is a single point. The new markings coming from the point insertion procedure is represented by
*
; the dashed line between the new markings indicates that they come from the same node.

More precisely, as shown in Figure
3
, let
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
be a moduli of
r
-spin surfaces, and
\text{bd}_{BI}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
be a boundary corresponding to an NS boundary node with twist
2h
at the illegal half-node. We can glue to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1}
, along the boundary
\text{bd}_{BI}
, another moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
which has a boundary
\text{bd}_{AI}
diffeomorphic to
\text{bd}_{BI}
. Note that
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{2}
is a moduli of possibly disconnected
r
-spin surfaces, obtained by first detaching the boundary node, then “inserting” the illegal twist-
2h
boundary marked point to the interior as a twist-
h
internal marked point. The boundary strata along which we glue the moduli spaces are called
spurious boundaries
.

By applying this procedure repeatedly we get a glued moduli (see
[
29
, §4.6]
). A point in the pre-glued space represents a disjoint union of graded
r
-spin surfaces, together with the combinatorial data of dashed lines connecting each pair of boundary marking and internal marking which appear together in the point insertion procedure. A point in the glued space represents an equivalence class of such objects under the equivalence relation induced by point insertion procedure.

In this paper, we will only do the “
{\mathfrak{h}}=0
point insertion” in
[
29
]
,
i.e.
, we will do point insertion at an NS boundary node
n
if and only if the twist of the illegal half-node of
n
is zero,
i.e.
, in the case
h=0
in Figure
3
.

2.4.1
(r,0)
-surfaces,
(r,0)
-graphs and moduli

We now more formally describe the objects of the pre-glued moduli space.

Definition 2.25

(
[
29
, Definition 4.3]
)
.

An
(r,0)
-surface is a collection of legal connected stable graded
r
-spin surfaces (the components) such that
\text{Fix}(\phi)\neq\emptyset
and all boundary twists are
r-2
, together with

1.

a bijection (denoted by dashed lines) between a subset
B^{PI}
of the boundary tails with twist
r-2
and a subset
I^{PI}
of the internal tails with twist
0
which are not contracted boundary nodes;

2.

markings on the set of unpaired boundary tails
B^{up}
and internal tails
I^{up}
which are not contracted boundary nodes,
i.e.
identifications
I^{up}=\{1,2,\dots,\lvert I^{up}\rvert\}
and
B^{up}=\{1,2,\dots,\lvert B^{up}\rvert\}
.

We require that, in the collection of surfaces, there exists no genus-zero stable graded
r
-spin disk with only two tails, where both of them are in
I^{PI}\sqcup B^{PI}
.

•

We say an
(r,0)
-surface is an
(r,0)
-disk (genus-zero) if all the connect surfaces in the collection are genus-zero, and the graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
, whose vertices are connected disks in the collection and there is an edge between two vertices if they contain a pair of points corresponding to a dashed line, is a connected genus-zero graph.

•

We say an
(r,0)
-surface is an
(r,0)
-cylinder (genus-one) if one of the following two condition holds.

–

All the connect surfaces in the collection are genus-zero, and the graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is a connected genus-one graph.

–

One of the connect surfaces in the collection is genus-one while all of the other connect surfaces in the collection are genus-zero, and the graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is a connected genus-zero graph.

Remark 2.26
.

The
0
in
(r,0)
refers to
{\mathfrak{h}}=0
in the notation of
[
29
,
30
]
.

The graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
mentioned above characterizes the topological type of an
(r,0)
-surface. Since each graded
r
-spin surface in the collection of an
(r,0)
-surface is associated with a graded
r
-spin graph, by assigning each vertex of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
the corresponding graded
r
-spin graph, and specifying the corresponding pair of tails for each edge of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
, we obtain the following combinatorial object, which can be viewed as a refined version of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
.

Definition 2.27

(
[
29
, Definition 4.7]
)
.

A
(r,0)
-graph

\mathbf{G}
consists of

•

a set
V(\mathbf{G})
of connected legal stable graded
r
-spin graphs with at least one open vertex or contracted boundary tail, and the boundary tails of them all have twist
r-2
,;

•

two partitions of sets

\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}\left(T^{I}(\Gamma)\backslash H^{CB}(\Gamma)\right)=I(\mathbf{G})\sqcup I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})

and

\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}T^{B}(\Gamma)=B(\mathbf{G})\sqcup B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})

such that all
a\in I^{PI}(\bm{G})
have twist
0
;

•

a set of edges (the
dashed lines
)

E(\mathbf{G})\subseteq\{(a,b)\colon a\in I^{PI}(\mathbf{G}),b\in B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})\}

which induces an one-to-one correspondence
\delta
between
I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})
and
B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})
;

•

a labelling of the set
I(\mathbf{G})
by
\{1,2,\dots,l(\mathbf{G}):=\lvert I(\mathbf{G})\rvert\}
and a labelling of the set
B(\mathbf{G})
by
\{1,2,\dots,k(\mathbf{G}):=\lvert B(\mathbf{G})\rvert\}
.

We require that

1.

there exists no
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
satisfying

H^{I}(\Gamma)\subseteq I^{PI}(\mathbf{G}),H^{B}(\Gamma)\subseteq B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})

and
\lvert H^{I}(\Gamma)\rvert+\lvert H^{B}(\Gamma)\rvert\leq 2;

We define an auxiliary graph (in the normal sense)
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
in the following way: the set of vertices of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is
V(\mathbf{G})
, the set of edges of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is
E(\mathbf{G})
; an element
(a,b)\in E(\mathbf{G})
corresponds to an edge between the vertices
\Gamma_{a}
and
\Gamma_{b}
, where
a\in T^{I}(\Gamma_{a})
and
b\in T^{B}(\Gamma_{b})
. We also require that

2.

the graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
is connected.

The genus of an
(r,0)
-graph
\mathbf{G}
is defined to be

g(\mathbf{G})=\sum_{\Gamma\in V(\bm{G})}g(\Gamma)+g(\hat{\mathbf{G}}),

where
g(\Gamma)
is the genus of graded
r
-spin graph
\Gamma\in V(\bm{G})
, and
g(\hat{\mathbf{G}})
is the genus of the auxiliary graph
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
.

In this paper, we exclusively focus on the genus-zero and genus-one case.

Definition 2.28
.

For a genus-one
(r,0)
-graph
\mathbf{G}
, we define a subset

I^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})\quad\text{ (respectively }B^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})\text{)}

consisting all the elements
x
in
I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})
(respectively
B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})
) such that the graph
\operatorname{Detach}_{(x,\delta(x))}\hat{\mathbf{G}}
(obtained by removing the edge in
\hat{\mathbf{G}}
corresponding to the pair
(x,\delta(x))
) is non-connected. These tails are generated in point procedures for separating boundary nodes.
We also define

I^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G}):=I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})\setminus I^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\quad\text{ (respectively }B^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G}):=B^{PI}(\mathbf{G})\setminus B^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\text{)},

which are tails generated in point insertion procedures for non-separating boundary nodes.

Moreover, we define a subset

I^{PI}_{sp,1}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq I^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\quad\text{ (respectively }B^{PI}_{sp,1}(\mathbf{G})\subseteq B^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\text{)}

consisting all the elements
x
in
I^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})
(respectively
B^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})
) such either
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{x}
, the connected component of
\operatorname{Detach}_{(x,\delta(x))}\hat{\mathbf{G}}
containing
x
, is genus-one, or one of the vertex of
\hat{\mathbf{G}}_{x}
is a genus-one
r
-spin graph. We also define

I^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G}):=I^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\setminus I^{PI}_{sp,1}(\mathbf{G})\quad\text{ (respectively }B^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G}):=B^{PI}_{sp}(\mathbf{G})\setminus B^{PI}_{sp,1}(\mathbf{G})\text{)}.

For any
\Gamma\in V(\bm{G})
, we define the set of roots on
\Gamma
to be

RT(\Gamma):=T(\Gamma)\cap(B^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G})\sqcup I^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G})\sqcup I^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G})\sqcup B^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G})).

(2.22)

Remark 2.29
.

For any genus-one
(r,0)
-graph
\mathbf{G}
and
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
, if
\Gamma
is a genus-one
r
-spin graph, then we always have
RT(\Gamma)=\emptyset
. For genus-zero
\Gamma
, there are two possibilities:

1.

RT(\Gamma)\subset I^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G})\sqcup B^{PI}_{nsp}(\mathbf{G})
is a two-element set;

2.

RT(\Gamma)\subset I^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G})\sqcup B^{PI}_{sp,0}(\mathbf{G})
is an one-element set.

Therefore, for any genus-zero vertex
\Gamma
of
\mathbf{G}
, we can regard it as a rooted or 2-rooted
r
-spin graph.

Definition 2.30
.

Let
\mathbf{G}
be an
(r,0)
-graph. Let
e
be an edge or a contracted boundary tail of some
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
. Since
T^{I}(\Gamma)\backslash H^{CB}(\Gamma)=T^{I}(d_{e}\Gamma)\backslash H^{CB}(d_{e}\Gamma)
and
T^{B}(\Gamma)=T^{B}(d_{e}\Gamma)
, we define the
smoothing
of
\mathbf{G}
along
e
to be the
(r,0)
-graph
d_{e}\mathbf{G}
obtained by replacing
\Gamma
with
d_{e}\Gamma
.

We say
\mathbf{G}
is smooth if all
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
are smooth stable graded
r
-spin graphs.
We denote by
{\text{GPI}}^{r,0}_{g}
the set of all genus-
g

(r,0)
-graphs, by
{\text{GPI}}^{r,0}_{g,B,I}
the set of all genus-
g

(r,0)
-graphs
\mathbf{G}
satisfying
I(\mathbf{G})=I
and
B(\mathbf{G})=B
, by
\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{g,B,I}
the set

\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{g,B,I}:=\{\mathbf{G}\in{\text{GPI}}^{r,0}_{g,B,I}\colon\mathbf{G}\text{ smooth}\}.

Definition 2.31
.

An isomorphism between two
(r,0)
-graphs
\mathbf{G_{1}}
and
\mathbf{G_{2}}
consists of a collection of isomorphism of stable graded
r
-spin graphs between elements of
V(\mathbf{G_{1}})
and
V(\mathbf{G_{2}})
, which induces a bijection between
V(\mathbf{G_{1}})
and
V(\mathbf{G_{2}})
, and preserves the partitions, dashed lines, and labellings.

For each
\mathbf{G}\in{\text{GPI}}^{r,0}_{g}
, let
\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}
be the group of automorphisms of
\mathbf{G}\in{\text{GPI}}^{r,0}_{g}
, then there is a natural action of
\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}
over the product
\prod_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
. We define

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}:=\left(\prod_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\right)\bigg/\penalty\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}

and (when
\mathbf{G}
is genus zero or one)

{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}:=\left(\prod_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}\right)\bigg/\penalty\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}

and

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}:=\left(\prod_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\right)\bigg/\penalty\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}.

Notice that for genus-zero
\mathbf{G}
we always have
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}={\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}

2.4.2
Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}

Let
\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}
be the Witten bundle over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
, we define the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{G}}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
to be

\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{G}}:=\left(\bboxplus_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}\right)\bigg/\penalty\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}.

Remark 2.32
.

In this paper, when we only consider genus-zero or genus-one
(r,{\mathfrak{h}})
-graphs
\mathbf{G}
. In these two cases the automorphism groups
\operatorname{Aut}\mathbf{G}
are always trivial. We denote by
\pi_{\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma}
the projection maps

\pi_{\mathbf{G}\to\Gamma}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}.

Let
o_{\Gamma}
be the canonical relative orientation of
\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\Gamma}
, we define the canonical relative orientation
o_{\mathbf{G}}
of
\mathcal{W}_{\mathbf{G}}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
by

o_{\mathbf{G}}:=(-1)^{\lvert E(\mathbf{G})\rvert}\bboxtimes_{\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})}o_{\Gamma}.

(2.23)

Observe that
o_{\mathbf{G}}
is independent of the order of the wedge product, since for each
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
we have

\dim{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\equiv\operatorname{rank}\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}\mod 2.

Definition 2.33
.

Given a finite set
I
of internal markings with twist in
\{0,1,\dots,r-1\}
and a finite set
B
of boundary markings with twist
r-2
, we define the moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
of
(r,0)
-surfaces with markings
B,I
to be

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{G}\in\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{0,B,I}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}.

(2.24)

For
g=0,1
, we also define the subspaces

{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{G}\in\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{0,B,I}}\!\!\!\!{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}

(2.25)

and

{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:=\bigsqcup_{\mathbf{G}\in\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{0,B,I}}\!\!\!\!{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}\,\,\,\subseteq\,\,{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}.

(2.26)

The Witten bundles with relative orientations over the connected components
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
of
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
induce the Witten bundle
\mathcal{W}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
with relative orientation.

2.4.3
Relative cotangent line bundles over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}

For any
i\in I
, there are three different notions of relative cotangent line bundles over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
.

1.

For any
\bm{G}\in\text{sGPI}^{r,0}_{g,B,I}
, let
\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}\in V(\bm{G})
be the unique vertex of
\bm{G}
such that
i\in I\cap T^{I}(\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}})
, we denote by
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
the line bundle pulled back from
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
via the projection
\pi_{\bm{G}\to\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
, and by
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
the line bundle defined in this way on all connected components.

2.

With the above notation, we denote by
\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
the graph obtained by forgetting all the inserted internal tails of
\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
in
I^{PI}(\bm{G})\cap T^{I}(\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}})
and by
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}\to\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
the forgetful morphism (if
\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
is unstable we formally take
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
to be a point). We denote by
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
the line bundle pulled back from
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
(if
\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
is unstable we formally take it to be a trivial complex line) via

\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}\to\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\circ\pi_{\bm{G}\to\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathcal{B}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}

and by
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
the line bundle defined in this way on all connected components.

3.

In the case
g=1
, with the above notation, we denote by
\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
the graph obtained by forgetting all the inserted internal tails of
\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
in
I^{PI}_{sp,1}(\bm{G})\cap T^{I}(\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}})
and by
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
the forgetful morphism (if
\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}
is unstable, again we formally take
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
to be a point). We denote by
\mathbb{L}^{*}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
the line bundle pulled back from
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}
via

\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}\to\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\circ\pi_{\bm{G}\to\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\hat{\mathcal{B}}\Gamma_{i,\bm{G}}}

and by
\mathbb{L}^{*}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
the line bundle defined in this way on all connected components.

The relation between these relative cotangent line bundles will be discussed is §
3.1
.

Remark 2.34
.

In
[
29
,
30
]
, the notation “
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{0,B,I}
” refers to what we denote by “
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{0,B,I}
” in this paper. We make this change of notation because in this paper we mainly work with the line bundles
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{0,B,I}
which ‘forget’ all the inserted points in
I^{PI}
.

2.4.4
Boundary strata and point insertion

For an
(r,0)
-graph
\bm{G}
,
we write

E(\bm{G}):=\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in V(\bm{G})}E(\Gamma)

and

H^{CB}(\bm{G}):=\bigsqcup_{\Gamma\in V(\bm{G})}H^{CB}(\Gamma).

For a set

S\subseteq E(\bm{G})\sqcup H^{CB}(\bm{G})
,
we can perform a sequence of smoothings (in any order, since they lead to the same result) and obtain an
(r,0)
-graph
d_{S}\bm{G}
. We set

\displaystyle\partial^{!}\bm{G}=\{\bm{H}\;|\;\bm{G}=d_{S}\bm{H}\text{ for some }S\},

\displaystyle\partial\bm{G}=\partial^{!}\bm{G}\setminus\{\bm{G}\},

\displaystyle\partial^{B}\bm{G}=\{\bm{H}\in\partial\bm{G}\;|\;E^{B}(\bm{H})\cup H^{CB}(\bm{H})\neq\emptyset\}.

For an
(r,0)
-graph
\mathbf{G}
, a boundary stratum of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
corresponds to a graph in
\partial^{!}\mathbf{G}
, or more precisely, a choice of
\Delta_{i}\in\partial^{!}\Gamma_{i}
for each
\Gamma_{i}\in V(\mathbf{G})
. In particular, a codimension-1 boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
for smooth
\mathbf{G}
is determined by a choice of
\Gamma\in V(\mathbf{G})
and a graph
\Delta\in\partial\Gamma
, where
\Delta
has either one contracted boundary tail and no edges, or exactly one edge which is a boundary edge. There are five different types of codimension-1 boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\mathbf{G}}
depending on the type of the (half-)edge of
\Delta
(or equivalently, the corresponding node of a surface
C\in\mathcal{M}_{\Delta}
):

CB

contracted boundary tails;

R

Ramond boundary edges;

NS+

NS boundary edges whose twist on the illegal side is greater than
0
;

AI

NS boundary edges whose twist on the illegal side is
0
, and the vertex containing the legal half-node only contains this half-edge and an internal tail
a\in T^{I}(\Delta)\cap I^{PI}(\mathbf{G})
;

BI

the remaining NS boundary edges whose twist on the illegal side is
0
.

Therefore, the codimension-1 boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
is a union of five different types of boundaries. Similarly, the codimension-1 boundary of
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
is also a union of such five different types of boundaries since
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}={\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\cap\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
, while
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
only has three types of boundaries since all type-NS+ and type-R boundaries are removed from
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
.

We will denote by
\partial^{CB}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
,
\partial^{R}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
,
\partial^{NS+}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
,
\partial^{AI}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
and
\partial^{BI}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
the corresponding boundaries in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}
. We also write
\mathcal{Z}^{dj}_{\bm{G}}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\bm{G}}\setminus{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}_{\bm{G}}
.

Remark 2.35
.

The abbreviation “BI” stands for “before-insertion”, while the abbreviation “AI” stands for “after-insertion”.

We claim that there is an one-to-one correspondence between the type-AI boundaries and the type-BI boundaries.

Theorem 2.36

(
[
29
, Theorem 4.12]
)
.

For fixed
I
and
B
, there is a one-to-one correspondence
PI
between the type-BI boundaries and the type-AI boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
(respectively
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
or
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
). Two boundaries paired by the correspondence
PI
are canonically diffeomorphic, and this diffeomorphism can be lifted to the Witten bundles (when
g=1
we restrict to
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{1,B,I}
) and the relative cotangent line bundles restricted to them. Moreover, the canonical relative orientations of the Witten bundles on the paired (spurious) boundaries induced by the canonical relative orientations are opposite to each other.

Remark 2.37
.

When
g=0
, the diffeomorphism a type-BI boundary and a type-AI boundary extend to a diffeomorphism between their closure. When
g\geq 1
, we can extend such diffeomorphism to a surjective morphism from the closure of type-AI boundary to the closure of type-BI boundary, which is not necessary injective on the higher-codimensional boundary strata. Such surjective morphism still pulls back Witten bundles and relative cotangent line bundles on the closures.

D_{2}

:
An example of two
(r,0)
-disks lying on two boundaries
\text{bd}_{BI}
and
\text{bd}_{AI}
paired by
PI
. The component
C_{1}^{BI}
of the
(r,0)
-disk on the left has a type-BI node, while the component
D_{1}^{AI}
of the
(r,0)
-disk on the left has a type-AI node. The shaded irreducible component only contains a legal half-node (corresponding to a type-AI node) and an internal tail in
I^{PI}
.

Figure 5
:
An example of two
(r,0)
-cylinders paired by
PI
, where the left one has a non-separating BI-type boundary node, and the right one has an separating AI-type boundary node, and a dashed line connecting a boundary tail and a internal tail on the same connected component.

Let
\sim_{PI}
be the equivalent relation induced by the correspondence
PI
on the boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
(and its restriction to the boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
or
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
), where
p_{AI}
in the closure of an AI-type boundary is equivalent to
p_{BI}
in the closure of the corresponding BI-type boundary if
p_{BI}
is the image of
p_{AI}
under the surjective morphism in Remark
2.37
. Theorem
2.36
shows that we can glue
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
,
{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{QM}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
along the paired boundaries and obtain piecewise smooth glued moduli spaces

\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\big/\sim_{PI},

\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:=\overline{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\big/\sim_{PI}\,\,\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}

and

\widetilde{\mathcal{PM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}:=\overline{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}\big/\sim_{PI}\,\,\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}.

The objects parametrized by
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
are called
reduced
(r,0)
-surfaces
in
[
29
]
, they are equivalence classes of
(r,0)
-surfaces under the relation induced by
\sim_{PI}
. Note that
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
and
\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
have only boundaries of type CB, R, and NS+, while
\widetilde{\mathcal{PM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
has only boundaries of type CB.

The Witten bundles and the relative cotangent line bundles
\mathbb{L}_{i}
,
\mathbb{L}^{\prime}_{i}
or
\mathbb{L}_{i}^{*}
over the different connected components of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
can also be glued along the same boundaries into a glued Witten bundle
\widetilde{\mathcal{W}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
and glued relative cotangent line bundles
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
,
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{\prime}_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
or
\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}^{*}_{i}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},0}_{g,B,I}
with canonical complex structure.

Remark 2.38
.

In the case
r=2
and only NS insertions the Witten bundle is a trivial zero rank bundle. In this case the idea of gluing different moduli spaces to obtain an orbifold without boundary is due to Jake Solomon and the first named author
[
28
]
.

By Theorem
2.36
and the fact that the glued relative cotangent line bundles over
\widetilde{\mathcal{M}}^{\frac{1}{r},{\mathfrak{h}}}_{g,B,I}
carry canonical complex orientations, we obtain the following theorem:

Theorem 2.39
.

[
29
, Theorem 4.13]

All bundles of the form

\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}\widetilde{\mathbb{L}}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},{\mathfrak{h}}}_{g,B,I}\text{ or }\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}^{\prime}}}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},{\mathfrak{h}}}_{g,B,I}\text{ or }\widetilde{{\mathcal{W}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i=1}^{l}{\widetilde{\mathbb{L}^{*}_{i}}}^{\oplus d_{i}}\to\widetilde{\mathcal{QM}}^{\frac{1}{r},{\mathfrak{h}}}_{g,B,I}

are canonically relatively oriented.

2.5
Closed extended FJRW theories

In
[
5
,
4
]
it was observed that a certain extension of Witten’s
r
-spin theory plays an important role in the open
r
-spin theory. This
closed extended
r
-spin theory
is defined just like the usual genus
0
closed
r
-spin theory, only that we allow one marking to have a negative twist of
-1.
It was first observed in
[
20
]
that, as in the usual closed setting with only non-negative twists, also here
R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S}
is an orbifold vector bundle in genus zero, so Witten’s class
c_{W}
can be defined by as
c_{W}=c_{top}((R^{1}\pi_{*}\mathcal{S})^{\vee})
. The
closed extended
r
-spin correlators
are

\left\langle\prod_{i\in I}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},\text{ext}}_{0}:=r\int_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{0,I}}\hskip 0.0ptc_{W}\cap\psi_{1}^{d_{1}}\cdots\psi_{n}^{d_{n}}.

(2.27)

All these numbers are calculated in
[
5
]
. They satisfy the following relations.

Theorem 2.40

(
[
5
, Lemma 3.6]
)
.

For any
i_{1}\in I
and
j_{1}\neq j_{2}
in
I\setminus\{i_{1}\}
, we have the Topological Recursion Relation with respect to the
(i_{1},\{j_{1},j_{2}\})
:

\left\langle\tau^{a_{i_{1}}}_{d_{i_{1}}+1}\prod_{i\in I\setminus\{i_{1}\}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},\text{ext}}_{0}=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}R_{1}\sqcup R_{2}=I\setminus\{i_{1}\}\\
j_{1},j_{2}\in R_{2}\\
-1\leq a\leq r-2\end{subarray}}\left\langle\tau^{a}_{0}\tau^{a_{i_{1}}}_{d_{i_{1}}}\prod_{i\in R_{1}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},\text{ext}}_{0}\left\langle\tau^{r-2-a}_{0}\prod_{i\in R_{2}}\tau^{a_{i}}_{d_{i}}\right\rangle^{\frac{1}{r},\text{ext}}_{0}.

(2.28)

3
Relative cotangent line bundles and TRR multisections

The relative cotangent line bundles plays an important role in our construction of genus-one open
r
-spin theory.

3.1
Relation between different relative cotangent line bundles

Let
\Gamma
be a dual graph, let
\pi_{\Gamma}\colon\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
be the universal curve. We denote by
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}\subseteq\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
be the smooth locus, and by
\mathbb{L}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
the vertical cotangent line bundle,
i.e.,
the cokernel of
d\pi^{*}_{\Gamma}\colon\pi^{*}_{\Gamma}T^{*}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to T^{*}\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
. Then for
i\in T^{I}(\Gamma)
, denote by
\mu_{i,\Gamma}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
the section corresponding the internal marking marked by
i
, the relative cotangent line bundle at
i
can be defined as
\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma}:=\mu_{i,\Gamma}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
. We will omit
\Gamma
in the subscript and write
\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma}
as
\mathbb{L}_{i}
when there is no ambiguity.

Let
S^{I}\subset T^{I}(\Gamma)
and
S^{B}\subset T^{B}(\Gamma)
be two subsets of internal and boundary tails. Assuming
\Gamma
is stable after forgetting all the tails in
S^{I}
and
S^{B}
, we denote by
\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)
the graph after forgetting, by
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
the forgetful morphism, and by
\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\colon\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{U}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
the lift of
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
to the universal curve. According to
[
23
, §3.5]
, the morphism

d\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}^{*}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}^{*}T^{*}\mathcal{U}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\to T^{*}\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}

induces a morphism between the vertical cotangent lines

\mathfrak{t}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}\colon\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\to\mathbb{L}_{\Gamma}

on
U_{\Gamma}
.
The morphism
\mathfrak{t}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
is an isomorphism except on component of
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
that are contracted by
\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
, where it vanishes identically.

For
i\in T^{I}(\Gamma)\setminus S^{I}
, notice that the image of
\mu_{i,\Gamma}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to\mathcal{C}_{\Gamma}
always lies in the smooth locus
\mathcal{U}_{\Gamma}
, the morphism
\mathfrak{t}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
induce a morphism between the line bundles

\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}:=\mu_{i,\Gamma}^{*}\mathfrak{t}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}\colon\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}\to\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma}

over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
which vanishes at the locus
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
where
i
-th internal marking lies on a component contracted by
\widetilde{\operatorname{For}}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
. So either
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
is identically zero, or
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
can be written as finite union of normal-crossing codimension-two subspaces as

D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}=\bigcup_{\Delta\in\mathcal{G}_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma},

where
\mathcal{G}_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}\subset\partial\Gamma
consists of graphs such that
i
-th marking on a closed genus-zero vertex
v
, and there exist an separating internal edge
e\in E^{I}_{sp}(\Delta)
such that
d_{e}\Delta=\Gamma
, where the connected component
\Delta_{v}
of
\operatorname{Detach}_{e}\Delta
containing
v
is closed genus-zero, and all the tails in
T^{I}(\Delta_{v})
are
i
, the half-edge from
e
, or elements in
S^{I}
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the case
S^{B}=\emptyset
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}
is an isomorphism.

We assume that
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}\neq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, in this case, the vanishing locus
D_{i,S^{I}}
behave like a ‘divisor’,
i.e.,
it is a finite union of normal-crossing (real) codimension-two subspaces. Moreover, if we regard
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
as a section of
\mathcal{O}(D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}):=\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma}
, we have the following lemma.

Lemma 3.2
.

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth dual graph of genus 0 or 1 such that
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{0,\overline{B},I}
(or
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,\{\overline{B}_{1},\overline{B}_{2}\},I}
). The section
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
vanishes transversely at the codimension-two subspaces
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
for each
\Delta\in\mathcal{G}_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
.

Moreover, each
\Delta\in\mathcal{G}_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
consists of one closed vertex
v^{c}
and an open vertex with internal half-edges labelled by
I^{o}
. For any choice of
x\in\bar{B}
(or
x,y
in different
\bar{B}_{1}
and
\bar{B}_{2}
), the induced orientation by
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{x}_{0,\bar{B},I}
(or
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{x,y}_{1,(\overline{B}_{1},\overline{B}_{2}),I}
) and
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I},S^{B}}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
on
{\mathcal{M}}_{\Delta}
coincide with
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{x}_{0,\bar{B},I^{o}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{v^{c}}
(or
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}^{x,y}_{1,(\bar{B}_{1},\bar{B}_{2}),I^{o}}\boxtimes\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}_{v^{c}}
), where
\tilde{\mathfrak{o}}
are the orientations of moduli spaces of disks or cylinder constructed in
[
29
, §3.1]
.

Proof.

For a subset
T\in I
, we denote by
\Delta^{T}\in\partial{\Gamma}
the graph with two vertices
v^{c}
and
v^{c}
connected by a separating edge
e
, such that
H^{I}(v^{c})=T\sqcup\{h_{e}\}
, where
h_{e}
is the half-edge corresponding to
e
on
v^{c}
side.

We assume
S^{B}=\emptyset
. In the case
S^{I}=\{j\}
consists of a single element,
D_{i,\{j\},\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{\{i,j\}}}
is a smooth codimension-two subspaces. According to
[
23
, Lemma 3.43]
,
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,{i},\Gamma}
is a section vanishes transversely
D_{i,\{j\},\Gamma}
, and satisfy the orientation property.

Assuming we have proved the lemma for
\lvert S^{I}\rvert\leq n-1
, we now prove it in the case
S^{I}
consists of
n\geq 2
elements. Assuming
j\in S^{I}
is one of them, we write the line bundle
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{{S^{I}}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma}
as

(\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{i,\Gamma})\otimes\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}(\operatorname{For}_{\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}\otimes\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)})

and we can write (since the forgetful morphisms compose)

\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}=\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}\otimes\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}.

The section
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
vanishes at

D_{i,\{j\},\Gamma}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{\{i,j\}}},

while
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
vanishes at

\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{-1}(D_{i,S^{I}\setminus\{j\},\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)})=\bigcup_{T\subseteq S^{I},T\neq\{j\}}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{T\sqcup\{i\}}}.

We need to prove the transversality and property for the induced orientation.

The transversality and the orientation property at
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{\{i,j\}}}
is guaranteed by
[
23
, Lemma 3.43]
.

To prove the transversality and the orientation property at
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{{T\sqcup\{i\}}}}
for
T\neq\{j\}
, locally near
p\in{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta^{{T\sqcup\{i\}}}}
, we can take a small neighbourhood
p=(\operatorname{For}_{\{j\}}(p),0)\in U\times V\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, where
U\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
is a neighbourhood of
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}(p)
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
and
V
is a neighbourhood of 0 in the complex plane, where the restriction of
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
at
U\times V
is the projection to
U
. Then locally near
p
, the zero locus of
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
is
(\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Delta^{T\sqcup\{i\}})}\cap U)\times V
, and the induced orientation on it is the induced (by
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}
) orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Delta^{T\sqcup\{i\}})}
tensored by the canonical complex orientation of
V
. Then the lemma follows from the inductive hypothesis and the last items in
[
29
, Proposition 3.1 and Proposition 3.3]
.

∎

Remark 3.3
.

For any section
s
of a line bundle
K\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, there is a corresponding section
s\otimes\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
of
K\otimes\mathcal{O}(D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma})\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
with additional zero locus at
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
.

On the other hand, if we denote by
\mathcal{O}(-D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma})
the dual of
\mathcal{O}(D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma})
, then the dual section
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
has ‘poles’ at
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
. We can construct a section of
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
of
\mathcal{O}(-D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma})
which vanishes at
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
and coincides with
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
outside a small neighbourhood of
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
, whose induced orientation on
\mathcal{M}_{\Delta^{T\sqcup\{i\}}}
is the reverse of the orientation induced by
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}
.

In fact, when
\lvert S^{I}\rvert=1
,
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
is a smooth codimension-2 subspace, we can take a small tubular neighbourhood of
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
which is isomorphic to
D_{i,S^{I},\Gamma}\times\mathbb{D}_{z}
, where
\mathbb{D}_{z}
is a small disk in the complex plane parametrized by
z
, and
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
on this tubular neighbourhood is given by
1/z
under a suitable trivialization. We can take
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
to be
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
when
\lvert z\rvert\geq\varepsilon
for a small
\varepsilon
; when
\lvert z\rvert\leq\varepsilon
we take
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
to be
\bar{z}/\varepsilon^{2}
.

For
\lvert S^{I}\rvert\geq 2
, assuming we have constructed
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}
, we can construct
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}
inductively on
\lvert S^{I}\rvert
by setting

\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}=\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)\to\Gamma}^{\vee}\otimes\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{t}^{\prime}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S^{I}}(\Gamma)\to\operatorname{for}_{\{j\}}(\Gamma)}^{\vee}.

The transversality and orientation property follows from the same argument as in the proof of the above lemma.

3.2
Topological Recursion Relation multisections

In this subsection, we construct a specific multisection of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, called
Topological Recursion Relation (TRR) multisections
, for each smooth genus-zero dual graph
\Gamma
with one or two tail(s) as root(s), and for each genus-one smooth dual graph
\Gamma
. For graded
r
-spin graphs
\Gamma
, TRR multisections of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}^{1/r}
are the multisections pulled back from the spinless moduli via the forgetful morphism.

3.2.1
Genus-zero TRR section with respect to one root

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth genus-zero dual graph and
i\in T^{I}(\Gamma)
an internal tail, and
j\in T(\Gamma)
be a internal or boundary tail different from
i
. We regard
j
as a root of
\Gamma
and define a section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
in the following way.

For a smooth disk
[\Sigma]\in\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
, we denote by
z_{i}
the marking on
\Sigma
corresponding to
i
, and by
z_{j}
the (internal or boundary) marking on
\Sigma
corresponding to
j
. We identify the preferred half
\Sigma
with the upper half-plane and set

\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}\left(\Sigma\right)=dz\left.\left(\frac{1}{z-z_{j}}-\frac{1}{z-\bar{z}_{i}}\right)\right|_{z=z_{i}}\in T_{z_{i}}^{*}C=T_{z_{i}}^{*}(\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}).

(3.1)

This fibre-by-fibre definition glues to a section of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma}
. This section is easily seen to extend to a smooth global section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
over
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma},
: for a disk
\Sigma
that is not necessarily smooth,
let
\varphi_{C}
be the unique meromorphic differential on
C=\Sigma\cup\overline{\Sigma}
with simple poles at
\bar{z}_{i}
and
z_{j}
and at no other marked points or smooth points, such that the residue equals
1
at
z_{j}
and equals
-1
at
\bar{z}_{i}
, and the residues at every pair of half-nodes sum to
0
.
Then
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}(\Sigma)
is the evaluation of
\varphi_{C}
at
z_{i}\in C
.

If
j
is a boundary tail, in the simplest case
\Gamma=u_{0,1,1}
, a smooth genus-zero dual graph with only one internal tails
i
and one boundary tail
j
, the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u_{0,1,1},j}
is a non-vanishing section of
\mathbb{L}_{i}
over the zero-dimensional moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{0,0,2}}
. For general
\Gamma
, the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
is of the form

\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}=\tilde{t}_{i,u_{0,1,1}\to\Gamma}\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Gamma\to u_{0,1,1}}\mathfrak{s}_{i,u_{0,1,1},j},

where
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to u_{0,1,1}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{0,1,1}}
is the forgetful morphism forgetting all the tails except for
i
and
j
.

If
j
is an internal tail, in the simplest case
\Gamma=u_{0,0,2}
, a smooth genus-zero dual graph with only two internal tails
i,j
, the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u_{0,0,2},j}
is a section of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{0,0,2}}
which only vanishes at one endpoint (where the boundary of
\Sigma
contracts to a contracted boundary node) of the one-dimensional moduli space
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{0,0,2}}
. For general
\Gamma
, the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
is of the form

\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}=\tilde{t}_{i,u_{0,0,2}\to\Gamma}\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Gamma\to u_{0,0,2}}\mathfrak{s}_{i,u_{0,0,2},j},

where
\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to u_{0,0,2}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{0,0,2}}
is the forgetful morphism forgetting all the tails except for
i
and
j
.

Moreover, for a graph
\Delta\in\partial\Gamma
without internal edges, we denote by
v_{i,\Delta}\in V(\Delta)
the unique vertex containing the tail
i
and by
\pi_{\Delta\to v_{i,\Delta}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{i,\Delta}}
the projection. If
j\in T(v_{i,\Delta})
, then we have

\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi^{*}_{\Delta\to v_{i,\Delta}}\mathfrak{s}_{i,v_{i,\Delta},j}.

If
j\notin T(v_{i,\Delta})
, we denote by
r_{j}\in T^{B}(v_{i,\Delta})
to boundary half-edge ‘closest’ to
i
,
i.e.,
the tails
i
and
j
are on different connected component after we detach the edge of
\Delta
corresponding to
r_{j}
. In this case, we have

\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi^{*}_{\Delta\to v_{i,\Delta}}\mathfrak{s}_{i,v_{i,\Delta},r_{j}}.

When there is no ambiguity about the root, we will simply write
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
as
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma}
.

3.2.2
Genus-zero TRR section with respect to two roots

Let
\Gamma
be a smooth genus-zero dual graph and
i\in T^{I}(\Gamma)
be an internal tail,
j,k\in T(\Gamma)
be two tails different from
i
. We regard
j
and
k
as two roots of
\Gamma
and define a multisection
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j,k}
of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
in the following way.

The multisection
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j,k}
has two branches, each of them is of weight
1/2
. One branch is
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j}
, the other one is
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,k}
. The behaviour of
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j,k}
when restricted to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Delta}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
follows the behaviours of its branches.

When there is no ambiguity about the root, we will simply write
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma,j,k}
as
\mathfrak{s}_{i,\Gamma}
.

3.2.3
Genus-one TRR multisections

Let
u_{1,0,1}
be a smooth single-vertex open genus-one graph with one internal tail and no boundary tails, We first construct special multisections
\mathfrak{s}
,
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
and
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
of the universal line bundle
\mathbb{L}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u_{1,0,1}}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
. We parametrize
\mathcal{M}_{1,0,1}
by two real numbers
\tau
and
h
such that
0<h<\tau
, the point
(\tau,h)
represents a cylinder
(C,\Sigma,z_{1},\bar{z}_{1})
, where
C=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon 0\leq\operatorname{Im}z\leq 2\tau\}/<z\sim z+1>
,
\Sigma=\{z\in\mathbb{C}\colon 0\leq\operatorname{Im}z\leq\tau\}/<z\sim z+1>
, the mark point
z_{1}
and its conjugate
\bar{z}_{i}
are located at
h\sqrt{-1}
and
-h\sqrt{-1}
. At each point
(\tau,h)
,
dz+d\bar{z}
represents a vector in the fibre of
\mathbb{L}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,0,1}
over
(\tau,h)
, which is a cotangent vector dual to the tangent vector ‘parallel to the boundaries of
\Sigma
’. Hence we can use
dz+d\bar{z}
to represent a section of
\mathbb{L}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,0,1}
. We denote by
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
a multisection of
\mathbb{L}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,0,1}
, which has two branches, one branch is
dz+d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
, the other branch is
-dz-d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
. We also denote by
s^{x}_{1,0,1}:=\sqrt{-1}\cdot s^{y}_{1,0,1}
another multisection of
\mathbb{L}\to\mathcal{M}_{1,0,1}
; note that at each fibre, the branches of
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
are
\pm\sqrt{-1}\cdot(dz+d\bar{z})=\pm(dz-d\bar{z})
, which are cotangent vectors dual to the tangent vector ‘perpendicular to the boundaries of
\Sigma
’.

We consider the extension of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
(and hence
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
) to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
. Note that
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
has three types of codimension-1 boundaries as shown in Figure
6
.

1.

Boundary of the form
\Gamma_{sp}
, where
\Gamma_{sp}
is a graph consisting of two vertices: one genus-one open vertex
v_{sp}^{1}
and one genus-zero open vertex
v_{sp}^{0}
, where
v_{sp}^{1}
and
v_{sp}^{0}
are connected by an separating edge
e_{sp}
, and the unique marking (which is internal) corresponding to
z_{1}
is attached to
v_{sp}^{0}
.

The nodal cylinders in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
have a genus-one component and a genus-zero component. If we parametrize its genus-zero component by the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
, where the marked internal point
z_{1}
locates at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}
and the half-node corresponding to
e_{sp}
locates at
\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
. The limit of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
has two branches, under the above parametrization, one branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by
dz-d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
(
i.e.
dual to the section ‘pointing’ to the half-node), the other branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by the negative of the previous one,
i.e.

-dz+d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
.

2.

Boundary of the form
\Gamma_{nsp}
, where
\Gamma_{nsp}
is a graph consisting of one open genus-zero vertex
v_{nsp}
, which is connect to itself via a non-separating edge
e_{nsp}
. On
v_{nsp}
there are three half-edges in total: two boundary half-edges
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
of
e_{nsp}
, and an internal half-edge corresponding to the internal marked point.

The nodal cylinders in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}
have one genus-zero component. If we parametrize it by the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
, where the marked internal point
z_{1}
locates at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}
and the two boundary half-nodes corresponding to
h_{1}
and
h_{2}
locate at
\alpha,-1/\alpha\in\mathbb{C}
for
\lvert\alpha\rvert=1
. The limit of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}
has two branches, under the above parametrization, one branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by
dz-d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
(
i.e.
dual to the tangent vector ‘pointing’ to the angle bisector between directions toward
\alpha
and
-1/\alpha
), the other branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by the negative of the previous one,
i.e.

-dz+d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
.

3.

Boundary of the form
\Gamma_{cb}
, where
\Gamma_{cb}
is a graph consisting of one open genus-zero vertex
v_{cb}
, which is has a contracted boundary half-edge
e_{cb}
. On
v
there is one internal half-edge corresponding to the internal marked point.

The nodal cylinders in
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{cb}}
have one genus-zero component. If we parametrize it by the unit disk in
\mathbb{C}
, where the marked internal point
z_{1}
locates at the origin
0\in\mathbb{C}
and the contracted boundary node corresponding to
e_{cb}
locates at
\lambda\sqrt{-1}\in\mathbb{C}
for
0<\lambda<1
. The limit of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{cb}}
has two branches, under the above parametrization, one branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by
dz-d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
(
i.e.
dual to the tangent vector ‘pointing’ to the contracted boundary node), the other branch is represented (up to positive rescaling) by the negative of the previous one,
i.e.

-dz+d\bar{z}
with weight
\frac{1}{2}
.

Figure 6
:
The double cover of the basic moduli
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
,
i.e.,
the moduli space of pointed cylinders with labelled boundaries. We obtain
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
after quotient the involution exchanging the label of two boundaries,
i.e.,
the reflection along the vertical dashed axis.

The extensions of
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
,
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{cb}}
are the extensions of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
with an additional 90-degree rotation. We define a section
\mathfrak{s}
(up to rescaling by positive function) on the boundaries of
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
in the following way.

1.

On
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
, if we denote by
i
the unique internal tail (which is on
v^{0}_{sp}
) and by
h_{0}
the boundary half-edge on
v^{0}_{sp}
corresponding to
e_{sp}
, the multisection
\mathfrak{s}
is pulled back from the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,v^{0}_{sp},h}
of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{0}_{sp}}
via the projection
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v^{0}_{sp}}
. Under the parametrization as above,
\mathfrak{s}
(up to positive rescaling) has only one branch
dz-d\bar{z}
with weight 1, which is the dual to the section ‘pointing’ to the half-node.

2.

On
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}
, if we denote by
i
the unique internal tail and by
h_{1},h_{2}
the two boundary half-edges on
v_{nsp}
, the multisection
\mathfrak{s}
is pulled back from the section
\mathfrak{s}_{i,v_{nsp},h_{1},h_{2}}
of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}
via the (isomorphic) projection
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}
. Then
\mathfrak{s}
has two branches, each has weight
\frac{1}{2}
; under the parametrization as above, up to positive rescaling, one of them is
\alpha(dz+d\bar{z})
, the other one is
-\frac{1}{\alpha}(dz+d\bar{z})
, each of them is the dual of the tangent vector ‘pointing’ to one of the boundary half-nodes.
Notice that the restriction of such
\mathfrak{s}
at the corner
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}\cap{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
is consistent with the
\mathfrak{s}
defined on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
in the previous item. Also notice that
\mathfrak{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{nsp}}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}}
constructed in this way is symmetry in the sense that it is invariant under the automorphism
\operatorname{Exc}_{h_{1},h_{2}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}
exchanging the two boundary half-edges.

3.

On
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{cb}}
,
\mathfrak{s}
has two branches with weight
\frac{1}{2}
. On the banana-shape corner (see Figure
6
),
\mathfrak{s}
is the same as the limit of
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
; on the other corner (
i.e.,

{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{cb}}\cap{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
),
\mathfrak{s}
is already determined by its definition on
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma_{sp}}
, where the two branches coincide. In between these two corner, notices that the line determined by the two branches of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
divide the cotangent plane at the internal marking into two half-planes, we extend
\mathfrak{s}
from the corners to
\mathcal{M}_{\Gamma_{cb}}
in a way such that its two branches lie on different half-planes.

At any point in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
, the line determined by the two branches of
s^{y}_{1,0,1}
divide the cotangent plane at the internal marking into two half-planes, and the two branches of
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
lie in different half-planes. Moreover, at any point in
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
, the two branches of
\mathfrak{s}
either lie on in different half-planes, or they just coincide and lie on the dividing line. Therefore, there is a nowhere-vanishing homotopy between
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
and
\mathfrak{s}
on
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
, which means
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
and
\mathfrak{s}
have the same winding number in the boundary
\partial{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
. Notice that
s^{x}_{1,0,1}
is nowhere-vanishing in the entire
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
, we can extend
\mathfrak{s}
to a nowhere-vanishing multisection
\mathfrak{s}
on the entire
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}
.

For any smooth single-vertex genus-one graph
u
, assuming
i
is a internal tail of
u
, let

\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{1,0,1}

be the forgetful morphism forgetting all the half-edges except
i
. We define a multisection
\mathfrak{s}_{u}
of
\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
by

\mathfrak{s}_{u}:=\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,u_{1,0,1}\to u}\otimes\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}^{*}\mathfrak{s}.

For any
\Gamma\in\partial u
with out non-separating internal edges, assuming
i\in T^{I}(v)
for an open vertex
v\in V^{O}(\Gamma)
, there are three possibilities.

1.

v
is a genus-one vertex. In this case,
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}
is pulled back from
\mathfrak{s}_{i,v}
via the projection morphism
\pi_{\Gamma\to v}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v}
,
i.e.,

\mathfrak{s}_{i,u}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi^{*}_{\Gamma\to v}\mathfrak{s}_{i,v}.

(3.2)

This is because the composition

\operatorname{For}_{v\to u_{1,0,1}}\circ\pi_{\Gamma\to v}=\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}

implies
\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}^{*}\mathfrak{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi_{\Gamma\to v}^{*}\operatorname{For}_{v\to u_{1,0,1}}^{*}\mathfrak{s}
and
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,u_{1,0,1}\to u}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi_{\Gamma\to v}^{*}\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,u_{1,0,1}\to v}
.

2.

v
is a rooted genus-zero vertex with an boundary root
r_{v}
.
By construction we have

\mathfrak{s}_{i,v,r_{v}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,v^{0}_{sp}\to v}\otimes\operatorname{For}^{*}_{v\to v_{sp}^{0}}\mathfrak{s}|_{v^{0}_{sp}},

where
\operatorname{For}_{v\to v_{sp}^{0}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{sp}^{0}}
is the forgetful morphism forgetting all the half-edges of
v
except
i
and the root
j
, then

\operatorname{For}_{v\to v^{0}_{sp}}\circ\pi_{\Gamma\to v}=\pi_{\Gamma_{sp}\to v^{0}_{sp}}\circ\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}

implies

\mathfrak{s}_{i,u}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi^{*}_{\Gamma\to v}\mathfrak{s}_{i,v,r_{v}}.

(3.3)

3.

v
is a 2-rooted genus-zero vertex with boundary roots
r_{v}^{1}
and
r_{v}^{2}
.
By construction we have

\mathfrak{s}_{i,v,r_{v}^{1},r_{v}^{2}}=\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,v_{nsp}\to v}\otimes\operatorname{For}^{*}_{v\to v_{nsp}}\mathfrak{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}},

(3.4)

where
\operatorname{For}_{v\to v_{nsp}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}
is a forgetful morphism forgetting all the half-edges of
v
except
i
and the two boundary roots
r_{v}^{1}
and
r_{v}^{2}
. Although there are two such forgetful morphisms because both
v
and
v_{nsp}
have two boundary roots, (
3.4
) holds for both forgetful morphisms since
\mathfrak{s}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{v_{nsp}}}
is invariant under
\operatorname{Exc}_{h_{1},h_{2}}
.
Then

\operatorname{For}_{v\to v_{nsp}}\circ\pi_{\Gamma\to v}=\pi_{\Gamma_{nsp}\to v_{nsp}}\circ\operatorname{For}_{u\to u_{1,0,1}}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}

implies

\mathfrak{s}_{i,u}|_{{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}}=\pi^{*}_{\Gamma\to v}\mathfrak{s}_{i,v,r_{v}^{1},r_{v}^{2}}.

(3.5)

Observation 3.4
.

For an open genus-one, single-vertex graph
u
, since
\operatorname{For}_{I\setminus\{i\},\bar{B}^{1}\cup\bar{B}^{2}}^{*}\mathfrak{s}
vanishes nowhere, the zero locus of
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
coincides with the zero locus of
\tilde{\mathfrak{t}}_{i,i,I\setminus\{i\},\bar{B}^{1}\cup\bar{B}^{2},\Gamma}
, which is of the form
\bigcup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, where the set
\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)\subset\partial u
consists of graphs with two vertices: one genus-one open vertex
v_{\Gamma,o}
and one genus-zero closed vertex
v_{\Gamma,c}
, and the internal half-edge labelled by
i
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,c}
.

Similarly, for an open genus-zero, single-vertex, rooted graph
u
with root
r_{u}
, the zero locus of
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
is of the form
\bigcup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, where the set
\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)\subset\partial u
consists of graphs with two vertices: one genus-zero open vertex
v_{\Gamma,o}
and one genus-zero closed vertex
v_{\Gamma,c}
, and the internal half-edge labelled by
i
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,c}
, while the root
r_{u}
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,o}
.

For an open genus-zero, single-vertex, 2-rooted graph
u
with two boundary roots
r_{u}^{1},r_{u}^{2}
, the zero locus of
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{1},r_{u}^{2}}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
is of the form
\bigcup_{\Gamma\in\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
, where the set
\mathcal{G}_{i}(u)\subset\partial u
consists of graphs with two vertices: one genus-zero open vertex
v_{\Gamma,o}
and one genus-zero closed vertex
v_{\Gamma,c}
, and the internal half-edge labelled by
i
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,c}
, while at least one of the roots
r_{u}^{1}
or
r_{u}^{2}
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,o}
. Note that
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{1},r_{u}^{2}}
has two branches
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{1}}
and
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{2}}
with weight
1/2
, if only one of
r_{u}^{1}
or
r_{u}^{2}
is attached to
v_{\Gamma,o}
, then
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
is only the zero locus of one branch
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{1}}
or
\mathfrak{s}_{i,u,r_{u}^{2}}
; if both
r_{u}^{1}
and
r_{u}^{2}
are attached to
v_{\Gamma,o}
, then
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Gamma}
is is the zero locus of both branches.

Remark 3.5
.

When the graph
u
above is a (spinless) dual graph, the multiplicity of the zero locus in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
is one. When
u
is graded
r
-spin graded, the multiplicity of the zero locus in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{u}
is
r
. This is because there is additional
r
-fold isotropy present in
{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}^{1/r}_{u}
that was not present in the non-spin case, so the pulled-back section has order of vanishing
r
in the orbifold sense.

4
Definition of open
r
-spin correlators

4.1
Canonical multisections via tame perturbations of TRR

Let
\Gamma
be a connected smooth stable graded
r
-spin graph,
I
be a set of internal markings (not necessarily contained in
T^{I}(\Gamma)
), and
S\subseteq T^{I}\setminus I
a subset of twist zero internal tails.
Let
\bm{s}_{\Gamma}
be a multisection of

\mathcal{W}_{\Gamma}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I\cap T^{I}(\Gamma)}\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}{{\mathbb{L}}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}^{\oplus d_{i}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma},

we can write it in the form

\bm{s}_{\Gamma}=s^{\mathcal{W}}\oplus\bigoplus_{1\leq i\leq\lvert I^{d>0}\rvert}\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq d_{i}}s^{j}_{i},

where
s^{\mathcal{W}}
is a section of
\mathcal{W}
and each
s^{j}_{i}
is a multisection of
\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}{{\mathbb{L}}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}
. Recall that if
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is unstable we formally take
{\mathbb{L}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}
to be trivial complex line over the single point
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}
.

For any
\Delta\in\partial^{*}\Gamma
, we define a set
S_{\Delta}\subseteq S
by

S_{\Delta}:=\{a\in S\colon a\in T^{I}(u),u\in V(\Delta),\operatorname{for}_{S\cap T^{I}(u)}(u)\text{ needs to be contracted to stabilize }\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)\}.

We define
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
the graph obtained by forgetting all tails in
S_{\Delta}
, and contract all the unstable vertices repeatedly. Note that if
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is stable, then
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
is a stable graph in
\partial\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)
; if
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is unstable, then
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
is just the same as
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
.

Remark 4.1
.

If
\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)
is stable, then
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)}
is the image of
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
under the forgetful morphism

\operatorname{For}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)}
. We denote such restricted forgetful morphism by

\operatorname{For}_{\Delta\to{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}.

Remark 4.2
.

In the case
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
is stable, in the contracting procedure, if a vertex
v
with a tail labelled by
i\in I
is unstable and needs to be contracted, then the only possibility is that
v
is closed and has only one other half-edge connecting it to another vertex
v^{\prime}
. After contracting
v
, the marking
i
goes to
v^{\prime}
and replaces the original half-edge on
v^{\prime}
. After contracting all the unstable vertices in
\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)
repeatedly, the tail
i
is contracted to a unique vertex in
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
, denoted by
\chi(i)
, and stops being unstable. Similarly, the vertices
u\in V(\Delta)
not being contracted are one-to-one corespondent to the vertices of
\xi(u)\in\operatorname{for}_{S_{\Delta}}(\Gamma)
, and there is an natural identification between
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
and
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\xi(u)}
.

Notation 4.3
.

Let
\Delta
be a stable graded
r
-spin graph,
F_{\Delta}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}
be a vector bundle, and
F_{v}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v}
be vector bundle for each
v\in V(\Delta)
, such that

q^{*}(\bboxplus_{v\in V(\Delta)}F_{v})=\mu^{*}F_{\Delta}

where
q
and
\mu
are the morphisms in §
2.2.3
. If a multisecion
s_{\Delta}
of
F_{\Delta}
and multisections
s_{v}
of
F_{v}
satisfy

\mu^{*}s_{\Delta}=q^{*}(\bboxplus_{v\in V(\Delta)}s_{v}),

we will, by abuse of notation, write

s_{\Delta}=\bboxplus_{v\in V(\Delta)}s_{v}\quad\text{or}\quad s_{\Delta}=\bigoplus_{v\in V(\Delta)}\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Delta\to v}s_{v}

in the sense that
s_{\Delta}
is the gluing of
s_{v}
.

Definition 4.4
.

In the above notations, for an
r
-spin graph
\Delta\in\partial^{*}\Gamma
, we say the multisection
\bm{s}_{\Gamma}
is
decomposable at
\Delta
if

•

The restriction
\bm{s}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\Delta}
of
\bm{s}^{\mathcal{W}}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}\subset{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Gamma}
is of the form

\bm{s}^{\mathcal{W}}_{\Delta}=\bboxplus_{u\in V^{O}(\Delta)}\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}{{\,\maltese\,}}\bass_{u\in V^{C}(\Delta)}\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}},

(4.1)

where for
u\in V^{C}(\Delta)
,
\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}
is a coherent multisection (see Section
2.2.4
) of
\mathcal{W}_{u}\to\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{u}
such that
\overline{\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}}
is a multisection of
\mathcal{W}_{u}\to{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}={\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
; for
u\in V^{O}(\Delta)
,
\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}
is a multisection of
\mathcal{W}_{u}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
. Recall that, unless
u
is closed and has an anchor of twist
-1
, we have
\overline{\mathcal{R}}_{u}={\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{u}
and
\overline{\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}}=\bm{s}_{u}^{\mathcal{W}}
is a multisection of
\mathcal{W}_{u}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
.

•

For each
s^{j}_{i}
, its restriction
s^{j}_{i,\Delta}
to
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}
, is of the form

s^{j}_{i,\Delta}=\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Delta\to\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta\to u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}}\,\,s^{j}_{i,u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}},

(4.2)

under the natural identification between

\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Gamma\to\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}{{\mathbb{L}}}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S}(\Gamma)}\big|_{{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}}

and

\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Delta\to\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}\pi^{*}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta\to u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}}\operatorname{For}^{*}_{u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}\to\operatorname{for}_{S\cap T^{I}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S\cap T^{I}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})},

where
\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Delta\to\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}\colon{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\Delta}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}
is the forgetful morphism,
u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}
is the unique vertex of
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
containing the internal marking
i\in I
,
and
s^{j}_{i,u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}}
is a multisection of

\operatorname{For}^{*}_{u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}\to\operatorname{for}_{S\cap T^{I}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}\mathbb{L}_{i,\operatorname{for}_{S\cap T^{I}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}(u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i})}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u^{\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}_{i}}.

Furthermore, we say
\bm{s}_{\Gamma}
is
transversely decomposable at
\Delta
if for each vertices
v\in\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta
,

s_{v}:=\overline{\bm{s}_{\xi^{-1}(v)}^{\mathcal{W}}}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I\cap T^{I}(v)}\bigoplus_{1\leq j\leq d_{i}}s^{j}_{i,v}

is transverse to zero as a multisection of

\mathcal{W}_{v}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in I\cap T^{I}(v)}\mathbb{L}_{i}^{\oplus d_{i}}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{v}\cong{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{\xi^{-1}(v)}.

Remark 4.5
.

Intuitively speaking, if we ignore the subtlety caused by the assembling operator,
i.e.,
if we pretend the Witten bundles decompose as direct sum, then
s
is decomposable at
\Delta
means we can write

s_{\Delta}=\operatorname{For}^{*}_{\Delta\to\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}\bboxplus_{v\in\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta}s_{v},

and
s
is transversely decomposable at
\Delta
means every
s_{v}
is transverse to zero.

In the case
S=\emptyset
, we always have
\mathcal{T}_{S}\Delta=\Delta
. In this case,
let
I^{d>0}_{\Gamma}:=\{i\in T^{I}(\Gamma)\colon d_{i}\geq 1\}
be a subset of
T^{I}(\Gamma)
, let
\gamma\colon\{1,2,\dots,\lvert I_{\Gamma}^{d>0}\rvert\}\to I_{\Gamma}^{d>0}
be a bijection, we regard it as an order (also denoted by
\leq_{\gamma}
) on the set
I^{d>0}_{\Gamma}
.
To simplify the notation, we refer to the multisections

s_{\gamma(1)}^{1},s_{\gamma(1)}^{2},\dots,s_{\gamma(1)}^{d_{\gamma(1)}},s_{\gamma(2)}^{1},\dots,s_{\gamma(2)}^{d_{\gamma(2)}},\dots,s_{\gamma(\lvert I^{d>0}\rvert)}^{1},\dots,s_{\gamma(\lvert I^{d>0}\rvert)}^{d_{\gamma(\lvert I^{d>0}\rvert)}}

as

t_{1},t_{2},\dots,t_{D},

where
D=\sum_{i\in I}d_{i}
, and we set
t_{0}:=\bm{s}^{\mathcal{W}}
. We also set

T_{s}:=\bigoplus_{m=0}^{s}t_{m}.
We define a map
\nu\colon\{1,2,\dots,D\}\to I_{\Gamma}^{d>0}
in the way that
t_{m}
is a multisection of
\mathbb{L}_{\nu(m)}
. If
t_{m}=s^{j}_{\nu(m)}
is decomposable at
\Delta
, for any
u\in V(\Delta)
, we set
t_{m,u}=s^{j}_{\nu(m),u}
if
u
is the unique vertex of
\Delta
such that
\nu(m)\in T^{I}(u)
, and formally set
t_{m,u}=0
as a section of the rank-zero bundle if
\nu(m)\notin T^{I}(u)
.

Definition 4.6
.

With the above notations, for a smooth stable genus-one
r
-spin graph
\Gamma
, in the case
S=\emptyset\subseteq T^{I}\setminus I
, we say
\bm{s}_{\Gamma}
is
\gamma
-tame perturbation of TRR
if

1.

For all
\Delta\in\partial^{*}\Gamma
,
\bm{s}_{\Gamma}
is decomposable at
\Delta
.

2.

For all
\Delta\in\partial^{*}\Gamma
and all
u\in V(\Delta)
and
0\leq j\leq D
, the multisection

{T_{j,u}}:=\overline{\bm{s}^{\mathcal{W}}_{u}}\oplus t_{1,u}\oplus t_{2,u}\oplus\dots\oplus t_{j,u}

of

E_{j,u}:=\mathcal{W}_{u}\oplus\bigoplus_{i\in T^{I}(u)\cap\nu(\{1,2,\dots,j\})}\mathbb{L}_{i}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}

is transverse to zero.

3.

For all open vertex
u\in V(\Delta)
as above, if
\nu(j)\in T^{I}(u)
, there exists an open neighbourhood
K_{j,u}
of the zero locus (see Observation
3.4
)
\left(\bigcup_{\Lambda\in\mathcal{G}_{\nu(j)}(u)}{\overline{\mathcal{M}}}_{\Lambda}\right)\cap{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
of
\mathfrak{s}_{\nu(j),u}
in
{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
, such that for any
p\in{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}\setminus K_{j,u}
, we have
t_{j,u}(p)=\mathfrak{s}_{\nu(j),u}(p)
, where
\mathfrak{s}_{\nu(j),u}
is the TRR multisection of
\mathbb{L}_{\nu(j)}\to{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
(with respect to the rooted or 2-rooted structure of
u
if
u
is genus-zero) defined in §
3.2
.

4.

If
\rk E_{s,u}>\dim{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
for an open vertex
u\in V(\Delta)
and
\nu(s)\in T^{I}(u)
, then
T_{s-1,u}
vanishes nowhere on
K_{s,u}\subseteq{\overline{\mathcal{PM}}}_{u}
.

5.

If
